The Cognitive Brain

3 The Cognitive Brain

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why can a robot recognize what to do yet still be too late to act?

Learned models are what let a physical machine handle objects and scenes that no engineer enumerated, and they are also the largest and least predictable computation the machine carries. A model that recognizes the right object still has to move its weights through an onboard processor that sensing and control also depend on, and a larger model that recognizes more takes longer to answer. While it computes, the world keeps moving, so a correct answer can arrive after the conditions that justified it have changed. Predicting a sequence of future motions keeps the actuators supplied between model updates, but it cannot make those motions respond to observations the model has not yet received, and a proposal that arrives on time can still be wrong in ways the model’s own confidence does not reveal. An engineer who treats the learned model as a function that returns actions cannot size the processor, set the rates at which proposals are renewed, or say how long the machine may keep moving on an answer that has gone stale. Because motion beyond the causal boundary cannot be recalled, every learned proposal must remain a request that an independent permission path can refuse in time.

Learning Objectives
  • Compare classical analytical pipelines and learned representations on coverage, sensory robustness, and formal verifiability
  • Calculate the memory wall bound on a model’s fresh-evaluation rate (\(\ProposerEvaluationRate \le \SustainedMemoryBandwidth / \WeightMemory\)) under the embodied batch size 1 constraint, and explain why weight-only quantization shortens the bandwidth-bound decode but not the compute-bound prefill
  • Estimate key-value cache expansion from multi-camera tokenization, hidden dimensions, and retained history windows
  • Explain how action chunking (\(\ActionChunkHorizon\)) amortizes off-chip weight transfers without improving closed-loop reaction latency, and why that gap requires a second, smaller proposer
  • Compare autoregressive, one-pass, and iterative action decoders by the sequential evaluations and off-chip transfers one chunk costs
  • Apply replanning period, inference latency, and target drift to size an action chunk for a dynamic target
  • Evaluate the interface between a slow and a fast proposer by what the permission path can check against its own evidence
  • Diagnose cognitive failure modes: semantic hallucinations, uncalibrated softmax overconfidence, perceptual chattering, and expired intent leases
  • Identify the trajectory, timestamps, and validity information a proposer must hand to independent real-time verification
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

3.1 The Embodied Brain

The body’s limit records tell the permission path what it must refuse, but they cannot say what the machine should do next. That choice belongs to the Brain, the deliberative layer that interprets incoming sensor telemetry and proposes what the physical body should execute next. Over the past decade, physical AI architectures have transitioned from hand-authored geometric pipelines and finite-state machines to learned models, notably vision-language-action (VLA) architectures that map natural language instructions and camera pixels directly to candidate end-effector motions for objects no engineer explicitly modeled. This shift places the machine’s most computationally intensive workloads on its least predictable path. A deep neural proposer requires billions of operations, contends for shared memory bus bandwidth with low-latency sensing and control tasks, and predicts future actions for an environment that continues to evolve during inference. A physical constraint governs this interaction: a learned proposal remains useful only if it arrives, and remains physically valid, within the time window permitted by environmental dynamics. Furthermore, every candidate trajectory remains strictly a proposal. The hard-real-time permission path beneath the proposal boundary holds exclusive authority to validate and admit candidate motions before they actuate physical hardware.

The warehouse mobile manipulator illustrates this boundary concretely. The robot can operate within every torque, thermal, voltage, and braking limit of its body and meet the freshness deadline that the body’s budgets set for this same pick-station grasp. It can still fail to grasp an unfamiliar ceramic mug traveling along an active conveyor. Low-level limits safeguard physical actuators against structural and electrical damage, but they cannot interpret natural language instructions, recognize a target handle amid visual clutter, or plan an obstacle-free trajectory around a coworker’s arm. A learned vision-action model supplies this missing semantic interpretation, which must then be projected into metric Cartesian space using calibrated depth sensing and forward kinematics despite specular reflections, dynamic shadows, and transient occlusions. Because this proposer’s training coverage does not establish a closed-loop safety guarantee, and its execution demands must fit within the throughput, memory bandwidth, and thermal envelope of the onboard processor, every generated action sequence remains an unverified candidate until the permission path checks it against fresh telemetry and established safety envelopes.

↩︎ The mobile manipulator’s torque, thermal, voltage, and braking limits are established in The Five Physical Budgets.

This division exposes a timing dilemma, because physical dynamics do not pause while inference runs. As the vision model evaluates sensory frames, the conveyor belt advances, and the manipulator must continue tracking previously admitted waypoints without stalling or jerking. Deploying a larger foundation model can enhance open-world object recognition, but the resulting inference latency delays subsequent updates. Systems architects must therefore decouple three distinct temporal quantities: the observation frequency at which the proposer ingests fresh sensor frames, the trajectory horizon spanning the future waypoints contained within each proposal, and the validity window over which those predictions remain physically accurate. Synthesizing longer action horizons can bridge execution between inference cycles, but generating additional waypoints does not refresh the perceptual evidence that produced them.

The machine card of The running machine already commits the running machine to one answer, the two-speed brain architecture: a slow intent model revised at 2 Hz above a fast chunk policy renewed at 20 Hz. Both propose to a permission path that checks measured state on a 1 kHz tick (figure 1). The card states these rates; this chapter prices and checks that design. The slow intent model names a target. The fast chunk policy emits an action chunk, the short sequence of future setpoints from one inference that From Prediction to Action introduced, so the drives below have motion to track between evaluations (Zhao et al. 2023; Chi et al. 2025). Three claims govern this split. Because no single large model can renew its proposal fast enough to supply the arm, the fast policy must emit multi-step chunks, yet a chunk keeps the arm supplied without keeping the evidence behind it fresh. Consequently, the permission path can evaluate and refuse the slow model’s intent alongside the fast model’s candidate motion only when that intent arrives as a target checkable against independent depth measurements and time: a metric pose stamped with the capture time of the frame that generated it (section 3.5.3).

A first estimate shows why one model cannot do both jobs. If every fresh evaluation must stream all of a model’s weights from off-chip memory, its renewal rate can be no higher than the sustained bandwidth divided by the bytes of weights, a ceiling that falls in proportion to parameter count (figure 2). The intent model stores its weights as 4-bit integers (INT4, priced in section 3.4.2) rather than 16-bit floating point (FP16), which raises its ceiling fourfold. Both of the machine’s proposers still sit below their ceilings, because reading the input tokens in one pass through the model (the prefill) and then decoding the output add work that weight streaming alone ignores. Neither approaches the kilohertz rate at which the permission path and the servo loops run. The design problem is therefore deciding which computation can finish before the observation it started from goes stale. Section 3.4.1 derives the ceiling and the work above it; the figure is a preview of where the derivation lands.

Testing the two-speed design takes five steps. Classical pipelines built on hand-authored geometry and rigid state machines show why a learned proposer is needed at all, because open-world variability exhausts manual rule sets (section 3.2). Learned representations restore semantic breadth but pay for it in tokens, cache, and bus traffic (section 3.3). Pricing one fresh evaluation of the intent model on the application processor then exposes the memory wall and what quantization can and cannot do about it (section 3.4). Sizing the chunks that keep the arm supplied next separates the motion a chunk supplies from the time its evidence stays true (section 3.5). Because a proposal can arrive on schedule and still be wrong, the last step examines the failures that leave the permission path to refuse an on-time proposal (section 3.6).

3.2 The Classical Wall

In a fixed industrial workcell, a robotic gripper can approach the exact same fixture along an identical trajectory on every production cycle. Engineers can specify such repetitive motions through analytical geometry, rigid-body equations of motion, and deterministic state transitions. Classical control theory and traditional robotics flourish within this analytical formulation (Wiener 1948; Kalman 1960; Craig 2005). The engineering challenge sharpens, however, when the work moves from a rigid fixture to an unconstrained scenario, such as retrieving a randomly oriented mug from a moving conveyor belt. There the engineer cannot enumerate every combination of object geometry, pose, and physical contact in advance.

Wiener, Norbert. 1948. Cybernetics: Or Control and Communication in the Animal and the Machine. Wiley.
Kalman, Rudolph Emil. 1960. “A New Approach to Linear Filtering and Prediction Problems.” Journal of Basic Engineering 82 (1): 35–45. https://doi.org/10.1115/1.3662552.
Craig, John J. 2005. Introduction to Robotics: Mechanics and Control. 3rd ed. Pearson Prentice Hall.

1 End-Effector: The tool, gripper, or hand at the distal tip of a manipulator, the only part of the arm meant to touch the workpiece. Workspace and keep-out checks are written against its pose rather than against joint angles, which is why the proposal payload later in this chapter carries end-effector poses alongside joint targets.

2 Manipulator Equation: Classical control computes joint torques from \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) = \JointTorque\), with a configuration-dependent inertia matrix \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix\), Coriolis and centripetal terms \(\CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix\), and gravity \(\GravitationalTorqueVector\). Torque is linear in inertia, and a link’s inertia grows with the square of its distance from the joint, so an outstretched arm needs more torque for the same angular acceleration. Unmodeled contact, friction, or deformable objects break this rigid-body model, which is the gap learned proposers enter; Feedback linearization: Transforming nonlinear dynamics into decoupled double integrators shows how classical control inverts this model to compute torque.

Analytical kinematics and dynamics map target end-effector1 trajectories into joint angles, velocities, and actuator torques.2 Classical perception routines register incoming sensor point clouds against reference computer-aided design (CAD) models, while a synchronous finite-state machine steps the manipulator through sequential phases: approaching, aligning, grasping, and retracting. In a calibrated workcell, these analytical components reliably solve bounded manipulation tasks under explicit operational assumptions. Their computational latency and real-time execution bounds depend strictly on the chosen numerical solver, processor throughput, and contact dynamics model.

Where the operational environment remains structured and bounded, classical methods provide dependable behavior within their modeled operating envelopes. In a body shop (figure 3), the chassis sits in submillimeter tooling fixtures, and controlled lighting and interlock fencing constrain variation under stated fixture, access, and model assumptions. This controlled regime permits explicit physical modeling, servo timing analysis, and formal Lyapunov stability analysis.3 Establishing selected trajectory tracking error bounds (\(\lVert \TrackingErrorVector(\TimeInstant) \rVert \le \TrackingErrorBound\)), stability properties, or software execution guarantees requires justified model, implementation, and execution evidence; the absence of training data does not establish those properties.

3 Lyapunov Stability: Aleksandr Lyapunov’s 1892 method establishes stability near an equilibrium using a positive-definite, energy-like function whose derivative along the modeled dynamics is nonpositive; asymptotic convergence requires stronger decrease or invariance conditions (Slotine and Li 1991). Applying the method requires a justified closed-loop dynamics model and verified certificate conditions; a learned proposer does not acquire such a certificate merely from training. The barrier certificates of Safe Sets as Conditional Permission reuse the idea to keep the machine inside a safe set rather than at a point.

Slotine, Jean-Jacques E, and Weiping Li. 1991. Applied Nonlinear Control. Prentice Hall.

↩︎ The causal boundary, where an admitted command becomes physical work, is established in Defining the Causal Boundary.

When an embodied machine steps outside the structured factory cage into unstructured, open-world environments, however, this analytical paradigm hits the classical wall (Brooks 1991; Levine et al. 2018). Physical reality defies closed-form geometric modeling: real objects deform under contact, ambient illumination shifts unpredictably, and scenes present near-infinite visual and mechanical variety. A rescue test arena of tilted ramps and a victim dummy on a high shelf (figure 4) has none of the fixtures that made the welding cell analyzable: no part arrives at a known pose, and no template describes the terrain. In such unmapped, irregular settings, preauthored geometric templates and rigid state machines lose state coverage and fail to generalize.

Brooks, Rodney A. 1991. “Intelligence Without Representation.” Artificial Intelligence 47 (1–3): 139–59.
Levine, Sergey, Peter Pastor, Alex Krizhevsky, Julian Ibarz, and Deirdre Quillen. 2018. “Learning Hand-Eye Coordination for Robotic Grasping with Deep Learning and Large-Scale Data Collection.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 37 (4-5): 421–36.

Outside structured fixtures, the analytical perception-and-control pipeline breaks down whenever scenes vary beyond its hand-coded rules. A rigid CAD template fails on deformable cloth or irregular food items; glare or cast shadows can wipe out the depth edges needed to separate adjacent parcels; and an unexpected obstacle invalidates the guard conditions of a preauthored grasping state machine. Engineers can add templates, heuristic thresholds, and recovery branches to patch each failure, but the cases multiply faster than they can be tuned and tested.

Machine learning exits this trap by learning from diverse physical interactions and demonstrations rather than enumerating geometric rules by hand. Neural models extract graspable features and motion policies directly from high-dimensional sensor streams without a bespoke CAD model for every object. Those learned models do not replace the downstream analytical controller: deterministic feedback loops still enforce kinematic bounds and track trajectories at high rates. Instead, learning inserts a compute-heavy proposal stage whose memory traffic, arithmetic cost, and latency must fit inside the timing contract of the real-time controller.

3.3 Learned Representations

When commanded to pick an unfamiliar ceramic mug from a conveyor, a learned model relates the text prompt to visual features of the handle and proposes a plausible grasp region without an exact CAD model. VLA models combine multi-camera imagery, natural-language instructions, and proprioceptive robot state to produce candidate actions (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023; Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Chen, et al. 2023; Kim et al. 2024). These systems descend from early embodied language models such as SayCan and PaLM-E, which emitted high-level semantic plans rather than low-level motor commands (Ahn et al. 2022; Driess et al. 2023). The operational value of a VLA stems from broad generalization across diverse objects and ambient environments acquired during large-scale pretraining, rather than formal guarantees of correct scene interpretation.

Ahn, Michael, Anthony Brohan, Yevgen Chebotar, et al. 2022. “Do as I Can, Not as I Say: Grounding Language in Robotic Affordances.” Proceedings of the 6th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 205: 287–318.

Teleoperated demonstrations can likewise teach coordinated sensorimotor policies for tasks whose contacts and structural deformations resist analytic kinematic modeling. In a bimanual teleoperation workcell (figure 5), an operator moves low-cost leader arms while follower arms with parallel grippers reproduce the motion, and overhead and wrist cameras record what the followers saw. Those recordings become the training data for policies that later act alone (Zhao et al. 2023; Chi et al. 2025). The running machine’s chunk policy takes its size from ACT, which was trained on exactly such recordings. What such a policy learns from them is the action chunk itself, a short stretch of the operator’s motion predicted from one view. How demonstrations become a policy belongs to Policy Training; this chapter prices what the trained policy costs to run. Although learned representations tolerate sensory variations captured across training distributions, executing any proposed motion safely still demands calibrated spatial geometry and strict physical boundary enforcement. Empirical task success demonstrates capability over tested operating envelopes, but out-of-distribution shifts in illumination, surface friction, or payload dynamics can quickly induce behavioral drift or outright failure.

The computational cost of this capability depends directly on the model’s underlying representation. A transformer does not read pixels or words directly; it reads tokens, embedding vectors that each represent one slice of input, such as an image patch, a word fragment, or a joint angle. Across a VLA model, that token sequence flows from camera patches and prompt embeddings through self-attention to candidate action chunks (figure 6). Multi-view camera frames consume significant memory bandwidth during ingestion, historical context expands key-value (KV) cache footprints in memory, and the inference engine must produce subsequent actions before the physical state diverges from the initial observation. Evaluating an embodied learned model alongside a classical perception-planning pipeline requires assessing both task generalization and the end-to-end latency required to refresh action proposals.

To grasp the mug, the model turns camera images and a language instruction into candidate trajectories in three learned stages: tokenization, attention across the tokens in a backbone, and an action decoder that projects the backbone’s output into proposed motion. A proposal boundary separates all three from actuation, so every candidate action passes independent permission checks before it reaches the motor drives. Evaluating this pipeline from a systems perspective means tracing what each stage stores in memory, transfers across the memory bus, and delivers to the controllers below it:

  1. Sensory ingestion and spatial tokenization (the inputs). A vision transformer (ViT) frontend (Dosovitskiy et al. 2021) slices each incoming camera frame into a grid of non-overlapping pixel patches of size \(\VitPatchSize{\times}\VitPatchSize\) (typically \(14{\times}14\) or \(16{\times}16\) pixels). Each patch is linearly projected into a vector of width \(\HiddenDimension\), augmented with a position embedding (learned in the SigLIP and DINOv2 encoders that OpenVLA uses (Kim et al. 2024), sinusoidal in some other designs), and tagged with a learned camera identifier.

    In an illustrative three-camera configuration (left wrist, right wrist, and an overhead torso mount) capturing \(224{\times}224\) images, the image frontend prepares a buffer for each view. Each frame yields \((224 / 14)^2 =\) 256 spatial patches. Across 3 cameras, ingestion injects 768 visual tokens into the model’s sequence on every perceptual step. When concatenated with tokenized natural language commands (about 32 tokens) and proprioceptive joint state embeddings (about 16 tokens), the initial context runs slightly past 800 tokens, which the estimates below round to \(\SequenceLength \approx\) 800. In 16-bit floating-point (FP16) precision (\(2\,\text{bytes}\) per element) with hidden dimension \(\HiddenDimension =\) 4096, the token embeddings for one such step occupy \[M_{\text{embed}} = S \times d \times 2\,\text{bytes} = 800 \times 4096 \times 2 \approx 6.55\,\text{MB}\] a few megabytes, negligible beside the multi-gigabyte weights that section 3.4.1 prices. Tokens therefore cost the machine through the arithmetic every weight performs on each of them and through the cache they leave behind, not through the storage of the embeddings themselves. Neither 2D image coordinates nor internal attention patterns constitute metric depth. Projecting an end-effector into physical 3D space requires calibrated extrinsic camera matrices and depth telemetry.

  2. Multimodal alignment and attention (the backbone). For the illustrative backbone, the token sequence passes through \(\LayerCount =\) 32 transformer layers where standard multi-head self-attention computes pairwise token interactions (Vaswani et al. 2017). To relate each new token to the earlier ones, every layer keeps a key and a value vector for every token it has already processed, the KV cache. The arithmetic of attention grows with the square of the sequence length (\(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2 \HiddenDimension)\) FLOPs), but the memory the cache occupies grows only linearly with it, as equation 1 gives: \[\KvCacheMemory = 2 \times \LayerCount \times \HiddenDimension \times \SequenceLength \times \ElementStorageSize \tag{1}\] The estimate assumes full-width keys and values, meaning every attention head in every layer stores its own. For a 32-layer model with \(\HiddenDimension =\) 4096 using FP16 storage, each context token then claims 524,288 bytes, about 0.524 MB, of KV cache, so an observation step of several hundred tokens holds hundreds of megabytes.

    Whether the machine pays that charge more than once depends on what the model keeps between evaluations. Single-observation VLAs such as OpenVLA rebuild their context from each new frame and carry no cache across control steps, and the running machine’s intent model is one of them. Only a design that conditions on retained history rereads the cache on every step, and section 3.5.2 prices what retaining several seconds of history would cost. Such rereading is not free, because the processor uses a unified memory architecture (UMA), in which the CPU, the GPU, and the camera interfaces share one DRAM. Every byte of cache read is bus time taken from weight streaming and from the direct memory access (DMA) transfers that deliver sensor frames. Grouped-query attention (GQA) narrows the cache by letting several query heads share one key and value; cross-attention can instead limit which retained representations the decoder reads. Either saving depends on the architecture, and action chunking does not provide it.

    Attention weights are also not geometry. A strong weight between the language tokens “mug handle” and a visual patch does not establish a graspable affordance4 or collision-free clearance, and the KV cache does not keep track of an object while it is occluded. The formal patch-embedding, position-encoding, and attention formulations appear in Visual patch tokenization, attention, and 3D metric lifting.

  3. Specialized action decoders (the outputs). Instead of generating open-ended conversational text, the final hidden representation \(\ContextHiddenRepresentation \in \mathbb{R}^\HiddenDimension\) is routed to specialized action decoders. The running machine’s chunk policy proposes base velocity and arm targets together in one whole-body stream (section 3.5.2), so its decoder is a manipulation decoder whose outputs include the base. Separate navigation models that decode waypoints over a topological map of the building (figure 7) follow the same proposal rules but are not part of this machine. Manipulation (VLA) models diverge sharply in how they bridge semantic representations to physical motion:

    Discrete action decoders map each action dimension to a categorical token by dividing its continuous range into bins (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023; Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Chen, et al. 2023; Kim et al. 2024). Autoregressive decoders such as RT-2 and OpenVLA then generate those tokens sequentially, requiring one complete model evaluation per action dimension (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Chen, et al. 2023; Kim et al. 2024). However, discretization does not require sequential decoding. RT-1 predicts all action tokens simultaneously in a single forward pass, and its authors demonstrated that autoregressively conditioning each action dimension on preceding dimensions yielded no accuracy gains while more than doubling inference latency (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023).

    What separates modern decoders in systems practice is the computational cost of generating a trajectory chunk and the volume of model weights reread during inference. Three architectural families dominate implementations as of 2026:

    • Autoregressive tokens. Each token requires a separate sequential evaluation. With one token per action dimension per control step, predicting a chunk of \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) timesteps across \(\ActionDimension\) dimensions demands \(\ActionChunkHorizon \ActionDimension\) sequential evaluations, causing inference latency to scale with both horizon and degree of freedom. Per-step binning also breaks down at high control frequencies: consecutive continuous setpoints differ negligibly, producing runs of identical token targets from which next-token training learns almost nothing. Frequency-space action sequence tokenization (FAST) resolves this scaling bottleneck by applying a discrete cosine transform across each action dimension over the chunk horizon, representing the trajectory as a linear combination of cosine basis functions (Pertsch et al. 2025). Smooth physical motions concentrate energy in low-frequency coefficients, allowing aggressive truncation. FAST quantizes these coefficients and compresses them with byte-pair encoding, which repeatedly merges the most frequent adjacent pair of symbols into a new token, tying token counts directly to trajectory information complexity rather than \(\ActionChunkHorizon \ActionDimension\). On a 50 Hz bimanual task, FAST compressed 700 raw binned tokens to 53, achieving a 13.2× reduction (Pertsch et al. 2025). Token generation nevertheless remains sequential. The authors report an inference latency of approximately 750 ms per chunk for FAST-\(\pi_0\), compared to 100 ms for the flow-matching variant on an identical desktop GPU.
    • One-pass chunk heads. Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) regresses an entire trajectory chunk in a single feedforward decoder pass (Zhao et al. 2023). OpenVLA-OFT adapts an autoregressive VLA into a one-pass generator by passing placeholder action tokens through bidirectional self-attention and directly regressing continuous values for all action steps in parallel, increasing throughput by 26× on 8-step chunks (Kim et al. 2025). However, deterministic regression heads trained with mean squared error struggle with multimodal demonstration data: when presented with two equally valid paths around an obstacle, a naive regression loss averages them into an infeasible trajectory directly through the obstacle (Chi et al. 2025). Mode averaging when demonstrations split develops this mode averaging from the training side. ACT mitigates it by conditioning its decoder on a latent style variable, a code that records which of several demonstrated ways of doing the task a trajectory follows, learned with a conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE, Mode averaging and the decoders that avoid it).
    • Iterative action experts. Diffusion Policy formulates action generation as iterative denoising, starting a candidate chunk from random noise and repeatedly removing the noise a network predicts, adapting continuous diffusion processes originally developed for generative vision models (Ho et al. 2020; Chi et al. 2025). The \(\pi_0\) architecture pairs a 300M-parameter flow-matching5 action expert with a pretrained vision-language model, yielding 3.3B total parameters (Black et al. 2024). The large vision-language backbone processes perceptual observations once and caches its key-value activations; the action expert then generates the continuous trajectory chunk across 10 numerical integration steps while attending to that static cache. Each integration step is a forward Euler step. The expert starts the candidate from Gaussian noise, evaluates a learned velocity field that says which direction moves it toward a plausible action chunk, and moves the candidate a small distance that way; after 10 steps the candidate is the chunk. Each step requires a forward pass only through the lightweight action expert and its cross-attention reads into the cached backbone KV activations, avoiding any re-evaluation of the multi-billion-parameter vision-language backbone. Subsequent designs such as \(\pi_{0.5}\) and GR00T N1 employ this exact decoupling (Black, Brown, et al. 2025; Bjorck et al. 2025), with \(\pi_{0.5}\) combining FAST-token pretraining on the backbone with flow-expert execution at runtime.

    Across all three families, the continuous output forms a multi-step trajectory chunk: \[\PolicyActionWindow = [\CognitivePolicyAction_{t}, \CognitivePolicyAction_{t+1}, \dots, \CognitivePolicyAction_{t+\ActionChunkHorizon-1}] \in \mathbb{R}^{\ActionChunkHorizon \times \ActionDimension}\] Here \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) specifies the future prediction horizon in discrete setpoints and \(\ActionDimension\) denotes the degrees of freedom per action; section 3.5.1 states the formal definition (3.2) once the memory wall has shown why chunks are needed. Published horizons span from tens to hundreds of setpoints: Diffusion Policy predicts 16 steps and executes the initial 8 (Chi et al. 2025), \(\pi_0\) predicts 50 steps (Black et al. 2024), and ACT predicts 100 steps at 50 Hz (Zhao et al. 2023). The structural gap between prediction and execution horizons resurfaces in section 3.5.2 as the interface between asynchronous trajectory generation and real-time permission filtering. A trajectory chunk provides an open-loop sequence of future setpoints derived from a single sensory context, amortizing inference latency without incorporating intervening sensor observations. Section 3.5.1 evaluates the computational efficiency of these three decoder families on target silicon, while Mode averaging and the decoders that avoid it gives the discrete tokenization rule and details how generative decoders capture multimodal demonstration distributions without averaging conflicting trajectories.

  4. The proposal boundary (isolation from actuators). Whether the model plans navigation or manipulation, its output is an unprivileged candidate proposal strictly outside the proposal boundary (definition 1.3). Under the concrete machine model established in The Machine in Five Levels, the foundation model runs in unprivileged user space on the host application processor and has no direct memory-mapped access to pulse-width modulation (PWM) registers or motor coil current drivers. Instead, the Brain hands candidate motion, stamped with the capture times of the sensory frames that generated it, to a permission path whose kilohertz loops a slow inference cannot preempt or bypass. That path refuses proposals that would collide, violate joint limits, or drive the arm into a kinematic singularity (an arm configuration in which the joints lose the ability to move the end effector in some direction). Actuation Authority isolates this gating logic on a dedicated real-time safety microcontroller, completely segregated from the application processor hosting the learned model.

4 Affordance: Gibson’s term for the action possibilities an environment offers an agent, defined jointly by object properties and agent physical capabilities (Gibson 1979). A handle affords a grasp only when its physical dimensions and clearance accommodate the robot’s gripper geometry and kinematics. While self-attention weights across image patches highlight semantically relevant features such as a handle, they do not certify mechanical grasp stability or kinematic reachability, the failure mode section 3.6.1 analyzes.

Gibson, James J. 1979. The Ecological Approach to Visual Perception. Houghton Mifflin.

5 Flow Matching: A method that trains a neural network to predict a continuous velocity field carrying samples from Gaussian noise to the target data distribution along nearly straight paths (Lipman et al. 2022). Because those paths are straight, numerical solvers can generate samples in a handful of Euler steps, whereas older denoising diffusion models needed hundreds of sequential steps (Ho et al. 2020). On an embedded robot processor, the step count directly sets latency because each solver step rereads the action expert’s weights and cached visual context from off-chip memory.

Lipman, Yaron, Ricky TQ Chen, Heli Ben-Hamu, Maximilian Nicklas, and Matt Le. 2022. “Flow Matching for Generative Modeling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2210.02747.
Ho, Jonathan, Ajay Jain, and Pieter Abbeel. 2020. “Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 33: 6840–51.

The choice of sensory representation and action decoder dictates the memory traffic and latency profile of every control cycle. Maintaining historical token context accumulates KV cache overhead that consumes memory bus bandwidth, while running multi-billion-parameter backbones on resource-constrained embedded systems demands heavy weight streaming from off-chip DRAM. An embodied agent needs both semantic breadth and rapid trajectory renewal, and these costs suggest that one monolithic model on an embedded processor cannot deliver both. Whether it can is a question of numbers, and the place to get them is one fresh evaluation of the machine’s intent model on its own application processor.

3.4 The Cost of a Fresh Evaluation

At the pick station the mobile manipulator’s arm consumes a new setpoint every few tens of milliseconds, while the mug keeps moving along the conveyor. Whether one large model could supply those setpoints depends on how long one fresh evaluation of it takes on the application processor, and that time depends on which resource the evaluation exhausts first, the memory bus or the arithmetic units. The intent model’s evaluation is priced first, together with what quantization can buy back; section 3.5 then checks the motion buffer that the selected split relies on against the target’s drift. The single large-model cases below are alternatives used to expose the trade-off, not changes to the selected machine.

After ingestion (the tokenization of section 3.3), four learned stages turn those tokens into action proposals, and each hands the next a record that ages (figure 8). Across the proposal boundary, an isolated safety microcontroller (MCU) running the permission path on a 1 kHz tick decides whether candidate setpoints become admitted drive commands. These stages describe what the machine must compute, not how its networks are divided; the three learned stages of section 3.3 describe the inside of one transformer. On the running machine, the intent model recognizes the mug and names the target (perception and intent), a tracker carries the mug’s estimate from frame to frame (memory), and the chunk policy, reading its own camera frames, turns the target into setpoints (planning). Memory here means the belief the machine keeps about the world, not DRAM. The chapters of Part III, beginning with Sensor Perception, develop each stage in depth; here they matter only for what each hands downstream and how quickly that record goes stale.

Perception grounds what it recognizes in metric coordinates relative to the base, so that the mug has a 3D position and orientation (an \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) pose)6 and a graspable handle rather than an abstract semantic label; its output ages from the moment of capture. Memory carries those estimates through occlusion and must let each one expire when the evidence behind it does. Intent decomposes a task such as “take the ceramic mug off the conveyor and hand it to the coworker at the packing station” into subgoals, and because the mug moves while the intent model deliberates, it hands the planner a target that expires, an intent lease in the vocabulary of Part III. Planning converts that target into candidate setpoints for the base and the arm, the proposals the permission path will judge.

6 SE(3) Pose: SE(3), the special Euclidean group in three dimensions, is the set of rigid-body poses, a rotation plus a translation with six degrees of freedom; “special” excludes mirror reflections, which no rigid object can undergo. A pose can be compared with measured depth and aged from its capture time, while a semantic label such as “mug handle” can be neither, which is why the handoff to planning is a pose and not a label.

↪ The observation contract that dates each perception output from its capture epoch is developed in The Observation Contract.

↪ The intent lease and the deadlines that end it are developed in The Intent Lease Specification and Contract.

These stages, and the ingestion before them, all run on the application processor and draw on the same unified memory bus, which is where the machine’s cadence is decided. On an edge robotics system-on-chip (SoC), memory bandwidth, arithmetic rate, and power envelope are fixed allocations, and model weights, sensor transfers, and host computation share that bandwidth. A policy cannot budget the full bus for itself while assuming that new camera frames arrive without interference, and scaling the model up only draws harder against the same ceiling. The transfer bound below is therefore a lower bound on evaluation time. The worked comparison later uses an assumed sustained fraction of peak bandwidth. Contention for Shared Resources models how weight fetches, camera transfers, and telemetry writes queue for the shared memory controller.

↩︎ That every added layer of onboard compute draws against fixed memory bandwidth, electrical power, and thermal dissipation is established in The Four Bedrock Laws.

Published measurements on the machine’s own module show how far apart those costs sit before any of that arithmetic. The MLPerf Inference edge suite times one query at a time, its single-stream scenario, for a fixed set of reference models (Reddi et al. 2019). Vendor submissions for the 64 GB Jetson AGX Orin place those reference latencies directly against the three cadences of the running machine (figure 9).

Only three of the seven reference models answer inside the 50 ms chunk period: classification in 0.64 ms, question answering, and detection in 11.7 ms. A six-billion-parameter language model takes 4.2 s to produce one summary, 8× the 500 ms intent period, and the span from fastest to slowest is about 16,000×. The figure is a measured version of the two-speed argument. A language-model proposer on this module can revise intent only at a fraction of the rate the arm consumes setpoints. The same submission also shows that the cost is not fixed by the silicon alone. The language model’s latency was 10.2 s one round earlier on identical hardware, so the software stack sets the cost as much as the silicon does. Which bytes that software moves per evaluation is what the memory-wall analysis below quantifies.

3.4.1 The memory wall

A multi-billion-parameter model takes hundreds of milliseconds on a processor rated at tens of tera-operations per second. Its weights do not fit in on-chip static RAM (SRAM) or cache, so every fresh evaluation must stream them from off-chip low-power DRAM (LPDDR5) across a narrower shared bus. On-chip SRAM and caches can reuse weights and intermediate state without another external transfer only while the working set fits the memory allocated to that workload. Nominal chip-wide capacity does not guarantee that even a small action head stays resident when competing tasks share the processor. When operands cannot reach the arithmetic units as fast as those units can multiply them, memory bandwidth, not arithmetic speed, limits the update rate. That ceiling is the memory wall, and on a moving robot its cost is a delayed proposal while the body keeps traveling.

Consider a robot that generates continuous motor actions one discrete step at a time through an autoregressive foundation model, such as a multi-billion-parameter VLA. Its weights are nonresident, too large to stay in on-chip memory, so each token step streams them across the external DRAM bus. In single-token decoding with FP16 weights and request batch size \(\BatchSize = 1\), the dense weight projections become matrix-vector operations (\(\LayerOutputVector = \WeightMatrix\LayerInputVector\)), in which each fetched weight takes part in a single multiply-add before it is discarded. The bus, not the arithmetic, then sets the pace. The sustained memory bandwidth \(\SustainedMemoryBandwidth\) and model weight footprint \(\WeightMemory\) bound the maximum fresh-evaluation frequency, as equation 2 states: \[\ProposerEvaluationRate \le \frac{\SustainedMemoryBandwidth}{\WeightMemory} \tag{2}\] This bound must use the bandwidth actually available to the policy and the weights it transfers at the selected precision. It prices only the weight transfer. A fresh evaluation first reads its input tokens (image patches and the instruction) through the backbone in one pass, the prefill. That pass reuses every fetched weight across all of those tokens, so arithmetic rather than bandwidth can bind it. Notebook 3.1 prices both terms for the mobile manipulator’s intent model. When a decoder emits one token per action dimension, each action dimension requires another token; if each token revisits off-chip parameters, that decode latency scales linearly with degrees of freedom.

Single-token decoding at batch size 1 Servers can combine independent requests so that a weight matrix multiplies several input vectors at once, reusing fetched weights. A robot waiting for its next action token often has only one such request. Waiting for future observations to form a batch would add delay, and sequentially dependent action tokens cannot all be evaluated at once by an autoregressive decoder. This limits reuse in those projections. It does not make every operation in an embodied model a matrix-vector product. Image patches, multiple camera views, prompt tokens, and parallel action decoders can expose matrix-matrix work within one robot’s request. The one-use-per-fetch estimate therefore describes the single-token weight sweep, not the complete perception-and-action workload.

Whether a phase of the evaluation meets the memory wall or the arithmetic limit is decided by how much work it does per byte it moves, its arithmetic intensity (\(\ArithmeticIntensity\)), defined in equation 3 as: \[\ArithmeticIntensity = \frac{\text{FLOPs}}{\text{Bytes transferred from off-chip memory}} \tag{3}\] For the dense weight projections of single-token decoding at batch size 1, each a matrix-vector product \(\LayerOutputVector = \WeightMatrix\LayerInputVector\), counting weight traffic alone gives an optimistic estimate, because activation and context traffic add further bytes. In FP16 each weight occupies 2 bytes and participates in exactly one multiply-accumulate operation (2 FLOPs), so \[\HalfPrecisionGemvIntensity = \frac{2\,\text{FLOPs}}{2\,\text{Bytes}} = 1.0\,\text{FLOP/byte}\] The roofline model (Williams et al. 2009) turns that ratio into a bound (figure 10). It plots attainable throughput against arithmetic intensity as two roofs. Below a crossover, the ridge point, throughput is capped by a sloped roof equal to bandwidth times the intensity; above it, by a flat roof equal to the processor’s peak arithmetic rate. It is read the same way as the stopping-budget plot of Delay consumes stopping clearance, where delay travel and braking travel trade dominance at a crossover speed. Both plots show two regimes and the point at which one gives way to the other.

The mobile manipulator’s application processor is a Jetson AGX Orin module (The machine data sheet). In its 60 W maximum-performance power mode, its dense FP16 GPU Tensor Core ceiling is 43 TFLOP/s; the aggregate sparse INT8 rating cannot be used for this FP16 workload. Using the registry’s rounded peak memory bandwidth of 204 GB/s, the hardware ridge point occurs at: \[I_{\text{ridge}} = \frac{R_{\text{peak}}}{\text{BW}} = \frac{43 TFLOP/s}{204 GB/s} \approx 210.8 FLOP/byte\] For this weight-streaming example, \(\HalfPrecisionGemvIntensity = 1.0\,\text{FLOP/byte}\) is far below 210.8 FLOP/byte, so memory bandwidth sets the roofline ceiling. Attainable throughput is bounded by the peak bandwidth times \(\HalfPrecisionGemvIntensity\), or 0.204 TFLOP/s, about 0.47 percent of the dense FP16 peak; the sustained bandwidth that the notebook below uses lowers it further. That ratio is a workload throughput bound, not a measurement of how many cores are idle. Both roofs also assume the maximum-performance mode. A battery-powered base often runs a lower power mode, down to 15 W on this module, where lower clocks drop both the compute and the bandwidth roof; the chassis thermal budget sets the mode the machine can sustain.

Definition 3.1: Memory wall

Memory wall is the limit on a workload’s rate that arises when its operands cannot reach the arithmetic units as fast as those units could consume them, so that the bytes moved from off-chip memory, not the operations performed, set the time per evaluation.

  1. Significance: A proposer whose weights do not stay on chip must stream them at least once per fresh evaluation, which gives equation 2. On the machine’s application processor, the intent model’s 14 GB of FP16 weights at 142.8 GB/s sustained allow at most 10.2 Hz before any vision or decoding work, two orders of magnitude below the 1 kHz permission tick.
  2. Distinction: A compute bound yields to faster arithmetic; the memory wall yields only to fewer bytes per evaluation (fewer or narrower weights, or weights that stay resident on chip) or to more bandwidth. It is also distinct from memory capacity: a model can fit in DRAM and still be too slow to stream.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that a processor’s peak arithmetic rating predicts its batch-1 inference rate. Single-token weight streaming on this processor reaches only about 0.47 percent of its dense FP16 peak, so the rating overstates the attainable rate by orders of magnitude.

On the mobile manipulator’s application processor, then, the memory wall7 caps the intent model’s fresh-evaluation rate, before any vision or decoding work, far below the 100–1000 Hz at which stabilization, joint impedance control, and reflex-level collision avoidance run (notebook 3.1). Each of those loop rates is set by the physical time constant of the motion its loop must stabilize, a derivation carried out in Multi-Rate Cadences. A model renewed that slowly can say where the arm should go, but it cannot close the loop on moving mass, so it proposes waypoints and a faster loop below it tracks them. That conclusion is about timing only. The separate requirement that learned outputs remain proposals, even when they arrive in time, comes from the failures of section 3.6.

7 Wulf and McKee: Their short paper named the memory wall (Wulf and McKee 1995), observing that processor speed was improving far faster than DRAM latency, so that memory access would eventually dominate run time whatever the processor did. Caches postponed the wall for workloads that reuse their data, but a batch-1 model on a robot meets it directly. When each weight is used once per fetch, the bus, not the arithmetic, sets how often a fresh proposal can arrive.

Wulf, Wm. A., and Sally A. McKee. 1995. “Hitting the Memory Wall: Implications of the Obvious.” ACM SIGARCH Computer Architecture News 23 (1): 20–24. https://doi.org/10.1145/216585.216588.

Repeated off-chip transfers also consume memory-system energy. Their power cost depends on the DRAM, controller, access pattern, and transfer rate, so a bandwidth calculation alone does not establish battery drain or actuator heating. Whatever the processor dissipates is heat released inside the same sealed chassis as the joint motors, and it raises the ambient temperature against which Thermal capacity and insulation breakdown thresholds rates each motor’s continuous torque.

A fresh evaluation is not a single point on the roofline. Its prefill reuses each fetched weight across all of its input tokens and lands past the ridge, while its decode returns to the memory-bound point, one full weight sweep per token. For a decoder that emits \(\EmittedTokenCount\) tokens one after another, equation 4 adds the two phases: \[\ProposerInferenceTime \approx \max\!\left(\frac{\PrefillOperations}{\EfficiencyHw\,\PeakRate},\ \frac{\WeightMemory}{\SustainedMemoryBandwidth}\right) + (\EmittedTokenCount-1)\,\frac{\WeightMemory}{\SustainedMemoryBandwidth} \tag{4}\] Here \(\PrefillOperations\) counts the prefill’s operations, \(\PeakRate\) is the processor’s peak arithmetic rate at the activation precision, and \(\EfficiencyHw\) is the fraction of that rate the prefill achieves. The prefill cannot finish before its own weight sweep, hence the maximum; the prefill emits the first token, and each of the other \(\EmittedTokenCount - 1\) tokens costs one more sweep. Notebook 3.1 prices both phases for the machine’s intent model.

Prefill creates KV state that decode extends within one evaluation. Figure 11 follows a single observation through prefill, first-token sampling, and subsequent decode passes, then marks the boundary at which the running machine rebuilds its context.

Cache reuse avoids recomputing the prefix projections during that evaluation. It neither refreshes the captured observation nor turns token state into a spatial memory of the room. A model that deliberately retains observation history has a different memory and bandwidth obligation.

Napkin Math 3.1: Pricing one intent-model evaluation
Problem: The mobile manipulator’s intent model is a 7B-parameter vision-language model sized after OpenVLA (Kim et al. 2024), running on the application processor. Where OpenVLA decodes a step of end-effector motion, the machine’s model decodes the same number of tokens to name a target pose (section 3.5.3). How long does one fresh evaluation take, which resource binds each part of it, and what renewal rate can the machine therefore state?

Variables:

  • Parameters \(\Parameters =\) 7B in \(\LayerCount =\) 32 layers of width \(\HiddenDimension =\) 4096 (Datasheet). The decoder emits \(\EmittedTokenCount =\) 7 tokens, one per output dimension, one after another (Datasheet).
  • Input tokens \(\SequenceLength =\) 288: 256 patches from one \(224{\times}224\) view (Derived) plus 32 instruction tokens (Illustrative).
  • Dense FP16 peak \(\PeakRate =\) 43 TFLOP/s (Datasheet), of which the prefill achieves \(\EfficiencyHw =\) 50 percent (Illustrative).
  • Peak bandwidth 204 GB/s (Datasheet), of which 70 percent is sustained (Illustrative), so \(\SustainedMemoryBandwidth =\) 142.8 GB/s.
  • Stored weights: FP16 (2 bytes per parameter) or INT4 (half a byte), with activations kept in 16 bits (Chosen).

Math:

The memory wall. FP16 weights occupy 14 GB. If every evaluation streams them once, the bound above gives \[t_{\text{sweep}} = \frac{M_{\text{weights}}}{\text{BW}_{\text{mem}}} = \frac{14 GB}{142.8 GB/s} \approx 98.0 ms, \qquad f \le \frac{1}{t_{\text{sweep}}} \approx 10.2 Hz\]

The prefill binds on arithmetic. All \(\SequenceLength\) input tokens pass through the backbone together, costing two FLOPs per parameter per token (each weight multiplies one input value and adds the product to a running sum) plus a small attention term: \[O_{\text{prefill}} \approx 2 N S + 2 N_{L} d S^2 = 4.03 \times 10^{12} + 2.17 \times 10^{10} \approx 4.05 \times 10^{12}\ \text{FLOP}\] Each fetched weight now serves \(\SequenceLength\) tokens, so the intensity is about \(\SequenceLength \approx\) 288 FLOP/byte, beyond the 210.8 FLOP/byte ridge of figure 10. Arithmetic binds, and the weight sweep runs underneath it: \[t_{\text{prefill}} = \frac{O_{\text{prefill}}}{\eta_{\text{hw}}\,R_{\text{peak}}} = \frac{4.05 \times 10^{12}\ \text{FLOP}}{0.5 \times 43 TFLOP/s} \approx 188.5 ms\]

The decode binds on bandwidth. The prefill emits the first action token. Each of the remaining \(\EmittedTokenCount - 1 =\) 6 tokens is a matrix-vector pass that rereads every weight: \[t_{\text{FP16}} = t_{\text{prefill}} + (S_{\text{out}}-1)\,t_{\text{sweep}} \approx 189 ms + 6 \times 98.0 ms \approx 189 ms + 588 ms = 777 ms\] That is about 1.3 Hz.

INT4 weights shrink only the bandwidth term. Storing the weights in four bits cuts them to 3.5 GB and each sweep to 24.5 ms, but the prefill still multiplies 16-bit activations at the FP16 rate: \[t_{\text{INT4}} \approx 189 ms + 6 \times 24.5 ms \approx 189 ms + 147 ms = 336 ms, \qquad f \le 2.98 Hz\]

Sensitivity. Parallel decoding (Kim et al. 2025) moves the action tokens into the prefill (\(\SequenceLength + \EmittedTokenCount =\) 295) and removes the decode sweeps, giving 194 ms, only 2.9 percent inside a 200 ms period with the processor busy for all of it. Three camera views (\(\SequenceLength \approx\) 800) raise the INT4 total to 676 ms.

Result: At INT4 one evaluation takes about 336 ms. A published measurement of the same model and quantization on this processor reports 336 ms per frame (Franklin 2024), a gap of 0.1 percent. That agreement is closer than two illustrative factors deserve. It shows that the model has the right terms, not that \(\EfficiencyHw\) and the sustained fraction are exactly right. A 3 Hz rate would need a 333 ms period, so the machine runs its intent model at 2 Hz, one evaluation per 500 ms.

Systems insight: The binding resource depends on the phase. Prefill reuses weights across tokens and is compute-bound; decode rereads them per token and is bandwidth-bound (Pope et al. 2023). Weight-only quantization attacks only the second, so after INT4 the compute-bound prefill is most of the latency, and the next gain must come from fewer input tokens, lower-precision arithmetic that the processor’s kernels support, or a faster compute roof. Section 3.4.2 develops that choice and its accuracy cost.

3.4.2 Quantizing the intent model

The memory wall leaves two levers on the weight term, fewer weights or fewer bytes per weight. A smaller model may lose task recognition capability, which requires task evidence, so the selected design keeps the 7B-parameter model and pulls the second lever. Quantization stores each weight as a low-precision integer plus a shared scale, and reconstructs an approximate weight when the kernel needs it. In the common symmetric form, the weights are divided into groups of \(\QuantizationBlockSize\), each group keeps one 16-bit scale \(\QuantizationScale\), and each weight \(\FullPrecisionWeight_i\) becomes a \(\QuantizationBitWidth\)-bit integer \(\IntegerWeightCode_i\) (Jacob et al. 2018; Lin et al. 2024): \[\QuantizationScale = \frac{\max_{i \in \text{group}} |\FullPrecisionWeight_i|}{2^{\QuantizationBitWidth-1} - 1}, \qquad \IntegerWeightCode_i = \operatorname{clamp}\!\left(\operatorname{round}\!\left(\frac{\FullPrecisionWeight_i}{\QuantizationScale}\right),\, -2^{\QuantizationBitWidth-1},\, 2^{\QuantizationBitWidth-1} - 1\right), \qquad \ReconstructedWeight_i = \QuantizationScale\, \IntegerWeightCode_i\] Rounding moves each reconstructed weight \(\ReconstructedWeight_i\) at most \(\QuantizationScale/2\) from \(\FullPrecisionWeight_i\), so one large weight in a group coarsens the step for all the others; choosing which weights to protect from that error is where quantization methods differ. The stored size is \(\QuantizationBitWidth/8\) bytes per weight plus the scales’ share. At \(\QuantizationBitWidth = 4\) with the 128-weight groups that activation-aware weight quantization (AWQ) uses (Lin et al. 2024), the scales add 3.1 percent to the four-bit payload, which the half-byte-per-parameter figure used here leaves out.

Two families of quantization act on different terms of the latency. Weight-only quantization, the machine’s choice, restores each weight to 16 bits inside the kernel just before the multiply. It shrinks the bytes every decode sweep rereads, but the arithmetic still runs at the 16-bit rate, so the compute-bound prefill does not move. Weight-and-activation quantization makes both operands integers, so the multiplications themselves run on integer units and can shorten the prefill as well. It pays off only when three conditions hold: the processor has fast integer units for that precision, the runtime has kernels for the model’s layer shapes, and the activations tolerate the coarser grid. A format without a kernel is only a smaller file. Evaluating the intent model at three weight precisions with the prefill-plus-decode model of notebook 3.1 shows how closely the two-term estimate tracks measurements of the same model on the same module (table 1) (Franklin 2024).

INT4 shrinks the decode; the 189 ms prefill it cannot touch now dominates.

The computed and measured columns agree within 2.5 percent for the 8-bit format and 0.1 percent for INT4. The model underestimates the FP16 run by 7.5 percent, consistent with that run’s slower runtime, so the comparison supports the model’s two terms rather than its exact constants. The table also shows where weight-only quantization stops paying. Each halving of the bytes halves the sweep, but the 188.5 ms prefill is untouched, and at INT4 it is already 56 percent of the latency. Even weights that cost no bytes at all would leave the prefill, so no weight-only format can lift the intent model above 5.3 Hz. Beyond that ceiling, the gain must come from integer arithmetic in the prefill, fewer input tokens, or a processor with a higher compute roof.

The last column carries the cost. On this module the INT4 build’s action score fell from 95.3 percent to 90.1 percent (Franklin 2024). OpenVLA’s authors, measuring task success on a real robot with a desktop GPU, found a different pattern. INT4 weights kept 71.9 percent success against 71.3 percent for bfloat16, while INT8 weights through a general-purpose quantization library fell to 58.1 percent. The cause was time, not rounding. The library’s added quantization operations cost more than the saved bytes, so the INT8 policy ran at 1.2 Hz on the evaluation GPU, against the 5 Hz control rate of its training data. The robot therefore met each observation later than the demonstrations had (Kim et al. 2024). On the module an 8-bit format was nearly free and INT4 reduced the offline action score; on the desktop GPU the 8-bit build lost success through its slower loop and INT4 was nearly free. The two 8-bit results used different software and kernels, and the two studies scored different tasks, so the pair shows that a bit width alone does not predict the outcome; the quantization method, the kernels, the control rate they allow, and the task together do.

Franklin, Dustin. 2024. OpenVLA: Vision/Language Action Models for Embodied Robotics. NVIDIA Jetson AI Lab tutorial.

A quantized checkpoint is therefore a different model. It must be evaluated on the machine’s own contact tasks at the deployed precision, carry its own calibration, and be named as such by the model_id of the proposal payload (section 3.5.4). The footprint saving also has a systems value. Dropping from 14 GB to 3.5 GB of weights returns memory that retained context and perception also need, though how much of the computed latency saving survives when the cameras and the chunk policy share the bus must be measured under load. Even with that saving, quantization leaves the machine’s intent model at 2 Hz, one fresh proposal every 500 ms, while the arm below it consumes setpoints tens of times faster. No lever on the weight term closes a gap that large, and the gap raises two questions the weights cannot answer: how the arm stays supplied between evaluations, and how long whatever supplies it stays true.

Checkpoint 3.1: The memory wall and its levers

One fresh evaluation of a large proposer pays a compute-bound prefill and a bandwidth-bound decode, and each lever moves only one of them.

3.5 Chunk Supply and Evidence Validity

One INT4 evaluation of the intent model takes 336 ms. Over that interval the mug keeps riding the conveyor, and an arm tracking a new setpoint every few tens of milliseconds would have nothing to track if each proposal named a single setpoint. Predicting a sequence of setpoints per evaluation solves the supply problem but raises a second one that the memory wall never touched. Every setpoint in such a sequence descends from one observation, so the sequence can outlast the evidence behind it. Sizing those sequences for the machine’s two proposers leads to three further questions: how long the supply stays true, what the slow proposer should hand the fast one, and what a proposal must carry for the permission path to refuse it.

3.5.1 Action chunking

If a proposer emits only one future setpoint per evaluation and takes longer than one setpoint step to finish the next inference pass, the controller runs out of planned motion while the processor is still computing. To keep dense waypoints flowing despite slow full-model inference, modern physical AI policies employ action chunking (definition 3.2), predicting a multi-step trajectory segment in a single pass (Zhao et al. 2023; Chi et al. 2025). Each chunk crosses the proposal boundary as one packet, reusing one evaluation across several outputs, though it does not make the next fresh camera observation arrive any sooner.

Definition 3.2: Temporal action chunking

Temporal action chunking is the policy formulation that predicts an entire horizon of \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) future continuous actions \(\PolicyActionWindow = [\CognitivePolicyAction_{t}, \dots, \CognitivePolicyAction_{t+\ActionChunkHorizon-1}]\) in a single neural evaluation or diffusion rollout, amortizing parameter memory transfers across extended physical execution windows.

  1. Significance: Chunking divides the weight traffic per supplied setpoint by \(\ActionChunkHorizon\), 16 on the mobile manipulator, but leaves the delay before a new observation can change the motion at one renewal period plus one inference, 90 ms for its chunk policy. It does not make fresh observations or policy decisions arrive at the waypoint rate; a downstream controller tracks the accepted trajectory between model updates.
  2. Distinction: Unlike single-step policies \(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} \sim \pi(\cdot \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t})\), which condition only on the current observation and re-evaluate the full neural network at every control step, action chunking deliberately generates multi-step feedforward trajectories. It executes only the start of each before the next replaces it (receding-horizon execution), and it blends across overlapping chunks to reconcile slow neural deliberation with fast closed-loop control.
  3. Common pitfall: Executing action chunks open-loop, without smoothing across chunk boundaries or a check by the permission path. Without smooth blending across overlapping chunk boundaries, switching between independently generated horizons injects acceleration jumps and torque shocks that excite mechanical gearbox resonances.

Predicting a horizon8 of \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) actions per forward pass spreads the multi-gigabyte DRAM weight streaming cost over \(\ActionChunkHorizon \times \SetpointStepInterval\) seconds of physical execution. On the mobile manipulator’s application processor, a 7B FP16 model (14 GB) generating single-step actions at the 40 Hz setpoint rate would demand 40 \(\times\) 14 GB \(=\) 560 GB/s of memory bandwidth, 3.9 times the 142.8 GB/s it can sustain. A chunk of \(\ActionChunkHorizon =\) 16 waypoints cuts that demand to 35 GB/s, 24.5 percent of the sustained bandwidth, if one sweep supplies each full chunk. More frequent replanning, decoder work, and competing memory traffic consume additional bandwidth. The saving is in weight traffic per supplied waypoint, not an equal gain in arithmetic intensity: predicting a chunk adds decoder work, and its position on the roofline depends on the operations performed and the bytes actually transferred. Predicting a sequence also changes how a learned policy uses recent actions, so this supply calculation establishes only the weight traffic for the stated schedule, not that the policy attends to the right evidence or recovers from an unfamiliar contact.

8 Receding Horizon: Model predictive control optimizes over a fixed horizon, executes only the first part of the plan, and then replans from the newly measured state, so the horizon recedes as time advances (Mayne et al. 2000). A chunked policy is the learned analog and inherits the same rule: the unexecuted tail of a chunk is a buffer that the next evaluation may overwrite, never a commitment the machine owes to finish.

Mayne, David Q., James B. Rawlings, Christopher V. Rao, and Pierre O. M. Scokaert. 2000. “Constrained Model Predictive Control: Stability and Optimality.” Automatica 36 (6): 789–814. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0005-1098(99)00214-9.

Supply rate vs. closed-loop reaction latency On the running machine, the smaller chunk policy follows its own illustrative schedule, in which three quantities separate the rate at which setpoints reach the servos from the delay before a new observation changes a proposal:

  • Setpoint supply cadence (\(\ActionExecutionRate = 1/\SetpointStepInterval =\) 40 Hz, \(\SetpointStepInterval =\) 25 ms): The rate at which the trajectory queue delivers discrete waypoints to the joint servo loop.
  • Policy replanning rate (\(\ChunkRenewalRate =\) 20 Hz, period \(\ChunkRenewalPeriod =\) 50 ms): The cadence at which the smaller chunk policy begins an evaluation from fresh sensory frames. The period is chosen as a whole number of setpoint steps, here 2, so that every renewal begins on a setpoint boundary.
  • Closed-loop reaction latency (\(\ChunkReactionLatency = \ChunkRenewalPeriod + \ProposerInferenceTime =\) 50 ms \(+\) 40 ms \(=\) 90 ms): The worst sampling-phase wait plus the assumed inference time until a revised proposal is available, excluding further admission and physical response delay.

In this illustrative schedule, a disturbance immediately after capture can wait up to 90 ms for a revised learned proposal, before any additional admission and actuation delay. The independent permission path need not wait for that revision. Current qualified evidence, meaning measurements whose error bound and age have been established for this use (Spatial Error and Clearance Bounds develops how), can trigger a protective response while the policy computes. Its ability to detect the relevant obstacle and complete a feasible response needs its own timing and sensing evidence. Chunking keeps candidate waypoints available; it establishes neither that response nor its deadline.

Pricing the decoder families The three decoder families of section 3.3 differ in how they turn one observation into a chunk, and the machine’s processor prices that difference. Suppose the intent-sized backbone, with its deployed INT4 weights, produced one fresh chunk of \(\ActionChunkHorizon =\) 16 setpoints of 7 values each. Every family first pays the same compute-bound prefill of the observation, about 189 ms, and then the families part ways:

  • Binned autoregressive tokens need 112 sequential decode steps, each rereading the backbone weights, for about 2.9 s.
  • FAST tokens cut the sequence to about 30 tokens, the count its authors report per arm for a one-second chunk and therefore generous for this shorter chunk, about 899 ms, still sequential.
  • A parallel head folds every action into one longer prefill, about 264 ms.
  • A \(\pi_0\)-sized action expert of 600 MB in 16-bit weights integrates 10 steps. Each step rereads the expert and the backbone’s 151 MB of cached keys and values rather than the backbone weights, about 5.3 ms per step on bandwidth, for about 241 ms in all. Continuous trajectory diffusion and flow matching derives the noise-prediction and flow-matching objectives these experts train on.

The decoder choice spans 12× in latency on the same backbone, the cost that parallel decoding and action experts were introduced to remove (Kim et al. 2025; Black et al. 2024). In the expert design, weights of hundreds of megabytes far exceed the processor’s on-chip caches, so each refinement step pays another off-chip transfer. A head small enough to stay resident would change that term. Reducing the number of refinement steps shortens the decoder provided the proposals keep the required task quality. Neither change touches the prefill of a fresh observation. None of the four therefore renews within the chunk policy’s 50 ms period, because the prefill alone exceeds it. A large backbone that reads each observation fresh cannot also be the fast renewer on this processor, whatever its decoder. Deployed dual systems reach the same conclusion by assigning renewal to a separate, smaller model, as table 2 records, and the machine does the same with its ACT chunk policy.

No decoder renews within 50 ms; the prefill alone is too slow.

Whatever the decoder, chunking creates one more problem, chunk seam continuity. Because each action chunk is generated independently from rolling sensory observations, the tail of chunk \(\ActionChunkIndex\) and the head of chunk \(\ActionChunkIndex+1\) may show slight positional and velocity discrepancies. If a controller concatenated these chunks directly, the resulting step-discontinuities in acceleration would inject large jerk (\(\dddot{\GeneralizedCoordinates}\)) into the physical linkages, inducing mechanical shock, acoustic vibration, gear-tooth wear, and motor overcurrent faults. To reduce discontinuities between overlapping chunks, systems can implement temporal ensembling, blending overlapping trajectory predictions via exponentially weighted averaging:

\[\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} = \frac{\sum_{k=0}^{\EnsemblingWindowLength-1} \ChunkEnsemblingWeight_k \, \CognitivePolicyAction_{t}^{(\ActionChunkIndex)}}{\sum_{k=0}^{\EnsemblingWindowLength-1} \ChunkEnsemblingWeight_k}, \quad \ChunkEnsemblingWeight_k = \exp(-\EnsemblingDecayRate \cdot \ActionChunkIndex)\]

where \(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t}^{(\ActionChunkIndex)}\) is the action for step \(\ControlStepIndex\) predicted by the \(\ActionChunkIndex\)-th oldest of the \(\EnsemblingWindowLength\) chunks that cover step \(\ControlStepIndex\) (\(\ActionChunkIndex = 0\) is the oldest), and \(\EnsemblingDecayRate > 0\) is a tunable weighting rate. Following ACT, the oldest prediction receives the largest weight, which favors smoothness over responsiveness; a smaller \(\EnsemblingDecayRate\) lets new observations enter the blend faster (Zhao et al. 2023). The formula assumes that the policy is queried at every control step, as ACT does, so that \(\EnsemblingWindowLength = \min(\ControlStepIndex + 1, \ActionChunkHorizon)\). The mobile manipulator’s chunk policy renews every 2 setpoints instead, and because its 50 ms renewal period is a whole number of 25 ms steps, each new chunk starts on a setpoint boundary. Because imitation-learned policies are trained on recorded demonstrations to predict motion starting from the instant the camera frame was captured, the waypoints use capture-relative times rather than starting when inference finishes, so elapsed entries are not newly executed when a chunk arrives. The first setpoint is dated at capture, and each following interval advances by 25 ms, so the last interval reaches the capture-relative horizon. By the time the 40 ms evaluation completes and the chunk arrives, the first 2 entries are already past; the next 2 setpoints precede replacement. The same formula then applies with \(\ActionChunkIndex\) counting renewals rather than steps. A 16-setpoint chunk renewed every 2 setpoints gives at most \(\EnsemblingWindowLength =\) 8 overlapping predictions at any step. A renewal period that fell between setpoints would leave each chunk’s first action off the grid, and the blend would first have to interpolate every chunk onto common setpoint times. Below the proposal boundary, the permission path interpolates the admitted waypoints into a setpoint for every bus cycle, and the drives close their own velocity and current loops on those setpoints.

↪ The interpolation that turns admitted waypoints into per-cycle setpoints, and the fallback it executes when waypoints stop arriving, is developed in Rate interpolation and boundary fallback execution.

Each chunk runs 2 of its 16 steps; later chunks overlap the rest.

Ensembling has two limits. It averages, and the average of two valid predictions need not be valid, the same mode averaging that defeats a regression head. If an older chunk passes left of an obstacle and a newer one passes right, the blend steers between them (Black, Galliker, et al. 2025). It also ignores what executes while the policy computes. With the assumed 40 ms inference and 25 ms setpoints, the arm consumes 2 setpoints of the previous chunk before the new one exists, so no new prediction can change them. Real-time chunking treats those setpoints as committed. It freezes the part of the previous chunk that will execute during inference and has a flow or diffusion policy fill in the rest of the new chunk so that it agrees with that frozen prefix, rather than blending afterward (Black, Galliker, et al. 2025). The method arrives from the learning side at this chapter’s distinction, in which the committed prefix is motion already admitted and the tail is a buffer that the next evaluation may still rewrite.

↪ The chunk-prediction objective and the modal commitment it buys in training are developed in Action chunking and multi-step trajectory prediction.

Systems Perspective 3.1: The temporal ensembling jerk trap
Exponentially weighted temporal ensembling averages overlapping predictions, but averaging sampled setpoints alone guarantees neither continuous position nor continuous velocity between samples or at chunk boundaries. The interpolator on the permission path determines those properties. If successive chunks disagree in slope, a controller can receive a step in commanded velocity even when position remains continuous. In joint proportional-derivative (PD) tracking (\(\JointTorque = \ProportionalGainMatrix \TrackingErrorVector + \DerivativeGainMatrix \TrackingErrorRate\)), that velocity jump produces a finite torque step. With an illustrative derivative gain for one joint, the diagonal entry \(\PidDerivativeGain = 40\,\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\cdot\text{s/rad}\) of \(\DerivativeGainMatrix\), and an illustrative velocity discontinuity \(\FiniteChange \JointVelocity = 0.5\,\text{rad/s}\), the derivative term changes by \(\FiniteChange \CommandedJointTorque = \PidDerivativeGain \FiniteChange \JointVelocity = 20\,\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\). Dividing by the servo period would instead describe a torque slew rate, not a torque. Abrupt torque changes can excite mechanical resonances or exceed drive limits. Interpolation must match position, velocity, and acceleration at adjoining segments, the property called \(\Glyph{C}^2\) continuity, while enforcing the machine’s velocity, acceleration, and torque limits. Smoothness alone does not establish that the requested motion is feasible.

↪ The quintic spline that matches position, velocity, and acceleration across a chunk seam is developed in Quintic splines and C^2 boundary matching.

A chunked policy therefore has two rates that a single “action rate” conflates: the spacing of the waypoints it supplies and the interval between the fresh observations that change them. Supplying more waypoints from one sensory context does not refresh the observation on which they depend. A downstream controller can use current state measurements while tracking admitted waypoints between renewals, and its feedback corrects tracking errors within its capabilities, but a newly perceived obstacle still requires a timely checking or replanning response. The mobile manipulator’s own budget makes this distinction quantitative, first for supply and then for the time the evidence behind a chunk stays true.

3.5.2 Two proposers and the evidence horizon

Chunking reduces the weight traffic per waypoint, but the decoder pricing showed that each fresh context still waits for the large backbone’s prefill, whatever the decoder. That prefill floor and the weight-streaming ceiling of figure 2 explain why embodied models in the published literature split into two parameter regimes rather than converging on a single model size (figure 12). Across 1,526 language and robotics models with disclosed parameter counts in the Epoch AI catalog between 2018 and 2026 (1,484 language and 42 robotics models) (Epoch AI 2026), language models climbed continuously past hundreds of billions of parameters. Embodied vision-language-action backbones followed that curve upward, from RT-1 (35M) to GR00T N1 (2.2B), \(\pi_0\) (3.3B), OpenVLA (7B), Helix System 2 (7B), RT-2 (55B), and PaLM-E (562B), to inherit web-scale semantic and visual breadth (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023; Bjorck et al. 2025; Black et al. 2024; Kim et al. 2024; Figure AI 2025; Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Chen, et al. 2023; Driess et al. 2023).
