Alongside those backbones, a compact tier of visuomotor chunk policies and action experts, including ACT (80M), Octo-Base (93M), Helix System 1 (80M), and the \(\pi_0\) action expert (300M), stayed near \(10^8\) parameters (88× smaller than the machine’s 7B intent model) so that onboard processors can renew trajectories at tens of hertz (Zhao et al. 2023; Octo Model Team et al. 2024).

Driess, Danny, Fei Xia, Mehdi SM Sajjadi, Corey Lynch, Aakanksha Chowdhery, Brian Ichter, Ayzaan Wahid, et al. 2023. “PaLM-e: An Embodied Multimodal Language Model.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 8469–88.
Octo Model Team, Dibya Ghosh, Homer Walke, Karl Pertsch, Kevin Black, Oier Mees Nair, et al. 2024. “Octo: An Open-Source Generalist Robot Policy.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2404.08839.

This two-tier split, a slow intent model above a fast chunk policy, can now be budgeted on the warehouse mobile manipulator, and the chunks the fast policy supplies can be checked against how long they stay true. Budgeting that split also uses a term that the stopping budget of Delay consumes stopping clearance already charged, a lease, authority that lapses unless it is renewed. The motion the chunk policy proposes runs under a chunk lease, the longest an admitted chunk may keep driving the actuators without an admitted successor. Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget sizes that lease against the stopping budget, so that a late renewal is contained by that lease rather than by the length of the chunk. Notebook 3.2 applies the memory bound to the split and checks whether chunking alone could give the larger model the required renewal cadence.

Napkin Math 3.2: The renewal gap
Problem: Chunking spreads one weight sweep across many setpoints. Can it let the intent model renew proposals within the chunk policy’s 50 ms period, or does the machine need a second, smaller proposer, and how much of the shared bus do the two proposers then take?

Variables:

  • Intent model: 14 GB of FP16 or 3.5 GB of INT4 weights (Derived), an INT4 evaluation of 336 ms that decodes \(\EmittedTokenCount =\) 7 tokens (Derived, notebook 3.1), run at 2 Hz (Chosen).
  • Chunk policy: 80M parameters, ACT’s size (Datasheet), 0.16 GB in FP16 (Derived), renewed at 20 Hz (Chosen).
  • Chunk of \(\ActionChunkHorizon =\) 16 setpoints at \(\SetpointStepInterval =\) 25 ms (Chosen); sustained bandwidth \(\SustainedMemoryBandwidth =\) 142.8 GB/s (Derived).

Math:

Amortization. Counting weight traffic only, as section 3.5.1 did, and setting aside the prefill’s arithmetic and the other decode sweeps, one FP16 sweep that emitted a whole chunk would be shared by \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) setpoints: \[t_{\text{amort}} = \frac{t_{\text{stream}}}{H} = \frac{98.0 ms}{16\text{ setpoints}} \approx 6.1 ms\text{ per setpoint}\]

Renewal. A fresh chunk still waits for a whole INT4 evaluation, prefill and decode together, while the chunk policy streams its own weights in about a millisecond; the rest of its inference target goes to vision encoding and the decoder’s arithmetic: \[\frac{t_{\text{INT4}}}{T_{\text{chunk}}} = \frac{336 ms}{50 ms} \approx 6.7\text{ renewal periods}, \qquad \frac{0.16 GB}{142.8 GB/s} \approx 1.12 ms\]

Bus share. The INT4 intent model streams its weights \(\EmittedTokenCount\) times per evaluation, once under the prefill and once per remaining token (equation 4); the chunk policy streams its weights once per renewal: \[S_{\text{out}}\,M_{\text{INT4}}\,f_{\text{intent}} + M_{\text{chunk}}\,f_{\text{chunk}} = 7 \times 3.5 GB \times 2 Hz + 0.16 GB \times 20 Hz = 49 GB/s + 3.2 GB/s = 52.2 GB/s\]

Result: Amortization cuts the weight time per setpoint to 6.1 ms, but each fresh intent-model chunk arrives 6.7 renewal periods after its capture. The chunk policy’s sweep takes 2.2 percent of its period. Together the two proposers average 52.2 GB/s of weight traffic, 37 percent of the sustained bandwidth.

Systems insight: Amortization is a supply lever, not a renewal lever, so the machine renews with a second, smaller proposer. The average hides the timing. For 147 ms of every 500 ms, the intent model’s decode sweeps are modeled at the full sustained bandwidth, so a chunk-policy evaluation that overlaps them competes for the same bus, and its latency target holds only if it is measured under that contention. A late renewal is not free: the arm keeps moving on the previous chunk, and the chunk lease bounds how far.

The selected design follows. The 80M-parameter chunk policy uses ACT as its size reference and is assumed to be adapted to the mobile manipulator. It proposes base velocity and arm joint targets together at 20 Hz (a 50 ms replanning period), emitting waypoints spaced at \(\SetpointStepInterval =\) 25 ms (a 40 Hz supply rate), within a 40 ms p99 inference target that includes vision. The permission path interpolates admitted waypoints into per-cycle setpoints and the drives close their velocity and current loops at their own faster rates. The intent model revises its target at 2 Hz. That is a candidate architecture, not a measured timing result. Loaded execution must establish the inference target, and a percentile target still permits overruns; the chunk lease contains a missed renewal.

What retained history would cost The intent model as deployed reads one frame and keeps nothing between evaluations, so the budget above is complete for it. A variant that conditioned on several seconds of history would add memory pressure that the weights alone do not show, and the cost is worth knowing before anyone proposes one. The per-token charge from section 3.3, 0.52 MB of full-width FP16 keys and values across all 32 layers through equation 1, now scales with history. One synchronized set of 3 \(224{\times}224\) frames (3 \(\times\) 256 \(=\) 768 patch tokens) adds 402.7 MB. This is a standard multi-head attention estimate for a hypothetical multi-camera extension, not a measured cache footprint for the published OpenVLA checkpoint. Counting language and proprioception tokens along with the visual patches raises each step to \(\SequenceLength \approx\) 800 tokens, so each observation step adds 419.4 MB of KV cache. Retaining an unpruned 5 s historical context window at 10 Hz would yield 21.0 GB of KV cache. This hypothetical history exceeds the published OpenVLA checkpoint’s configured context window; the calculation exposes the storage cost of such an extension, rather than claiming that the checkpoint supports it. Combined with the intent model’s 14 GB of weights, this example claims 35.0 GB, about half of the application processor’s 68.7 GB (the module sold as 64 GB counts in binary gigabytes, \(64 \times 2^{30}\) bytes) before runtime overhead. Perception, mapping, and the chunk policy also need that memory. Even where memory capacity permits, reading a large cache is further traffic on that same bus. Bounding retained history therefore serves both capacity and timely renewal. Spatial pooling or cross-attention can reduce context traffic; fixed-horizon action output does not by itself bound the observation history retained by the backbone.

5 s of retained context outweighs the 14 GB of weights.

The split fixes how often each proposer renews. The moving target fixes how long each renewal stays true. At the pick station the arm takes the mug from a moving conveyor, and a chunk is useful only while the mug stays within the grasp tolerance \(\PositionalTaskTolerance\) of where the chunk’s evidence placed it, starting from the handle estimate’s initial error \(\InitialTrackingError\). Untracked, the belt’s steady speed \(\ConveyorSpeed\) uses up the margin \(\PositionalTaskTolerance - \InitialTrackingError\) within the freshness deadline \(\PositionErrorCeiling/\SpeedBound\) of Measurement Freshness, here with \(\PositionErrorCeiling = \PositionalTaskTolerance - \InitialTrackingError\) and \(\SpeedBound = \ConveyorSpeed\). A tracker removes that steady motion. It is the memory stage of the pipeline, re-estimating the mug’s position and velocity from each new frame, and a chunk computed from its estimate aims at where the mug will be if the belt keeps its speed. What that extrapolation cannot foresee is a change of speed after capture, the mug slipping on the belt at a residual acceleration \(\SlipAccelerationBound\). The chunk’s setpoints were fixed at its capture, so later frames do not correct them, and extrapolation over an evidence age \(\Time\) drifts by \(\tfrac{1}{2} \SlipAccelerationBound \Time^2\). Setting that drift equal to the margin gives the evidence horizon of the tracked mug, the longest time the evidence behind a chunk keeps the mug within tolerance, in equation 5: \[\EvidenceHorizon = \sqrt{\frac{2\,(\PositionalTaskTolerance - \InitialTrackingError)}{\SlipAccelerationBound}} \tag{5}\] When inference meets the stated target and no further admission delay is charged, a chunk is replaced one renewal period plus one inference after the capture it was computed from, the reaction latency \(\ChunkRenewalPeriod + \ProposerInferenceTime\) of section 3.5.1. That span must fit inside \(\EvidenceHorizon\), and the chunk must carry enough setpoints to bridge it, as equation 6 requires: \[\MinimumChunkSetpointCount = \left\lceil \frac{\ChunkRenewalPeriod + \ProposerInferenceTime}{\SetpointStepInterval} \right\rceil \tag{6}\] Together, equation 5 and equation 6 distinguish the setpoint buffer a chunk carries from the duration its evidence remains valid. Notebook 3.3 applies both bounds to the machine’s schedule.

Napkin Math 3.3: Buffer, not permission
Problem: The chunk policy renews every 50 ms, is assumed to need 40 ms per evaluation, and emits 16 setpoints 25 ms apart. Does the schedule depend on tracking the mug, how many setpoints must a chunk carry, and how much of each chunk may the arm execute on the evidence behind it?

Variables:

  • Conveyor speed \(\ConveyorSpeed =\) 0.20 m/s and residual slip acceleration \(\SlipAccelerationBound =\) 0.50 m/s² (Illustrative).
  • Grasp tolerance \(\PositionalTaskTolerance =\) 15 mm (Chosen) and initial handle error \(\InitialTrackingError =\) 3 mm (Illustrative), a margin of 12 mm.
  • Renewal period \(\ChunkRenewalPeriod =\) 50 ms, setpoint step \(\SetpointStepInterval =\) 25 ms, and \(\ActionChunkHorizon =\) 16 (Chosen); inference \(\ProposerInferenceTime =\) 40 ms, a p99 target that includes vision (Illustrative).

Math:

Untracked deadline. The belt alone uses up the margin in \[\frac{r_{\text{tol}} - e_0}{v_{\text{conv}}} = \frac{12\ \text{mm}}{0.20\ \text{m/s}} = 60\ \text{ms}\]

Tracked horizon. With the steady motion removed, equation 5 gives \[\tau_{\text{ev}} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 12\ \text{mm}}{0.50\ \text{m/s}^2}} \approx 219\ \text{ms}\]

Age at replacement and supply. equation 6 and the drift over the evidence age since capture give \[T_{\text{chunk}} + t_{\text{infer}} = 50 + 40 = 90\ \text{ms}, \quad \tfrac{1}{2} a_{\text{slip}} T^2 = \tfrac{1}{2} \times 0.50 \times (0.09\ \text{s})^2 \approx 2.0\ \text{mm}, \quad h_{\min} = \left\lceil \tfrac{90}{25} \right\rceil = 4\]

The whole chunk. Executed open-loop to its end, one chunk would carry the mug to \[H\,\Delta t_{\text{step}} = 16 \times 25\ \text{ms} = 400\ \text{ms}, \quad e_0 + \tfrac{1}{2} \times 0.50 \times (0.40\ \text{s})^2 = 3 + 40 = 43\ \text{mm} > 15\ \text{mm}\]

Sensitivity. Renewing at 10 Hz instead raises the age at replacement to 140 ms, still inside \(\EvidenceHorizon\), and \(\MinimumChunkSetpointCount\) to 6.

Result: Untracked, the mug leaves tolerance after 60 ms, before the first replacement at 90 ms, so this schedule depends on the tracker. Tracked, the evidence holds for about 219 ms: 4 setpoints bridge each renewal, and the 90 ms evidence age at replacement permits drift of only 2.0 mm. The 400 ms chunk outlasts its evidence by 181 ms.

Systems insight: The chunk is sized for supply, not for execution. Its tail beyond \(\EvidenceHorizon\) keeps setpoints flowing if a renewal slips, but it is not permission to finish the motion, because the drift it would carry the arm through cannot be taken back once the gripper closes on the wrong place. The permission path therefore ages each chunk’s evidence against \(\EvidenceHorizon\), and when renewal stops, the chunk lease, not the chunk length, bounds how far the arm may keep going.

On one clock, the schedule looks like figure 13. With direct replacement, each chunk is in force for only 50 ms before its successor replaces it, so motion computed from that capture ends when the capture is 90 ms old, inside the 219 ms evidence horizon. Temporal ensembling instead retains contributions from older chunks. A blend must exclude contributions whose evidence has expired and carry the oldest capture time among the contributions it retains; the direct-replacement timing strip does not bound the age of that blend. If the chunk policy overruns its inference target, the notebook’s assumption no longer holds, and if renewal stops altogether, a further setpoint remains eligible only while both its evidence and the active chunk lease remain valid. If the implementation uses temporal ensembling, it must filter expired contributions before blending and date the result from the oldest retained evidence; seam continuity and feasibility still require independent checks.

Under the stated assumptions, the conveyor calculation supplies the quantities needed to connect cognition to physical permission. One chunk covers 400 ms of motion, a new chunk is assumed to meet its 40 ms inference target, and the evidence stays within the stated grasp tolerance for about 219 ms. The Nervous System must combine these proposer limits with the body’s measured limits to establish its handoff record. That requires more information than a trajectory tensor alone. Timely delivery and usable evidence bound how long a proposal can remain eligible; they do not establish that the proposed grasp was correct.

3.5.3 Handing intent to the chunk policy

Whether the permission path can audit the slow intent model’s goal alongside the fast chunk policy’s trajectory depends on what crosses the interface between the two tiers. That two-tier split is not peculiar to this machine. Helix runs a 7B-parameter vision-language model at 7 to 9 Hz above an 80M-parameter policy at 200 Hz (Figure AI 2025), and GR00T N1 runs its vision-language model at 10 Hz above a flow-matching transformer at 120 Hz (Bjorck et al. 2025). Across these and other published designs (table 2), the reported rates bracket the machine’s own 2 Hz and 20 Hz. What separates them is less their rates than what the slow model hands the fast one, and that choice decides what the permission path can check.

Deployed systems use three kinds of interface between the two tiers. With a latent vector, Helix’s slow model passes a single continuous latent that conditions the fast policy (Figure AI 2025), and GR00T N1’s policy attends to token embeddings taken from an intermediate layer of its vision-language model (Bjorck et al. 2025). Because a latent is trained end to end, it carries whatever the fast policy finds useful and costs no decoding. Other architectures pass a language subtask: \(\pi_{0.5}\) first predicts the next subtask as text and then generates actions conditioned on it (Black, Brown, et al. 2025), while the orchestrating model of Gemini Robotics 1.5 sends its action model natural-language step instructions (Gemini Robotics Team 2025a). Still others emit an explicit geometric target, as in Gemini Robotics-ER, which predicts points, two- and three-dimensional boxes, trajectories, and grasps with coordinates in the scene (Gemini Robotics Team 2025b).

The permission path can check only a claim it can compare with its own evidence, and the three interfaces differ exactly there. A latent vector means something only to the network that reads it. No independent checker can tell whether it refers to the mug or to the photograph of a mug on a carton (section 3.6.1), and it carries no position that could age against the evidence horizon \(\EvidenceHorizon\). A text subtask can be checked against the task predicates the permission path has admitted, but “pick up the mug” names no location, so it can be neither compared with measured depth nor aged against drift. A geometric target allows both checks. It can be compared with calibrated depth and keep-out geometry, and aged from the capture time of the frame it came from. This geometric interface lets the machine’s intent lease be enforced, because that lease names a grounded target, never a path, and expires at the earliest of the time its evidence stays valid and the deadlines the permission path sets. With a latent or a text interface, the permission path can still check each chunk’s motion under the chunk lease, but the target behind that motion lacks this independent grounded-position check.

The mobile manipulator therefore has its intent model emit a target: a position, an orientation, and a gripper command, the same seven values OpenVLA decodes as a step of motion, together with the capture time of the frame it read. The timing shows why that target cannot stand alone. One evaluation takes 336 ms, longer than the 219 ms evidence horizon of the tracked mug, so the pose the intent model read is stale before its target arrives. The intent model therefore contributes which object to take and approximately where, and the tracker of section 3.5.2 supplies where that object is now. The tracker binds the target to its own latest estimate of the mug, and the 80M-parameter chunk policy aims at that fresh pose. The two leases then age against different clocks. The intent lease ages from the capture time of the tracker’s latest estimate and is renewed each time the tracker confirms the same mug, so it lapses when tracking is lost or a permission-path deadline passes. Each chunk carries the capture time of the oldest observation behind it, normally the frame the chunk policy itself read, and its evidence ages from that time against \(\EvidenceHorizon\).

The geometric bottleneck costs capability. A target pose cannot say how to fold a shirt or where to regrasp a cable, information a latent carries without a schema, so a checkable interface limits the tasks a single target can express. The machine accepts that limit in exchange for an intent the permission path can refuse. The same reasoning applies one level down to the chunk policy’s action space. Joint targets can be checked directly against joint limits and end-effector poses against workspace keep-out regions, which is why the proposal payload of section 3.5.4 carries both.

3.5.4 Information the proposer must supply

The conveyor budget gives each payload field a physical purpose. The receiver needs the candidate motion, the capture time from which its evidence ages, and the conditions limiting its validity. Bundling that information with delivery metadata defines the proposal payload rather than a new evidence record in the book’s record chain. Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts wraps this payload in the proposal header the permission path validates, and the Nervous System’s handoff record combines this supply with the body’s limits.

The proposal payload \(\ProposalPayload\) is the typed candidate motion that the unprivileged Brain sends to the real-time Nervous System, packaged with the capture time of its evidence, the time after which it lapses, and the provenance the receiver needs to check it: \[\ProposalPayload = \langle \EvidenceCaptureInstant, \ProposalExpiryInstant, \ActionChunkHorizon, \SetpointStepInterval, \ProposedJointPositionSequence, \ProposedJointVelocitySequence, \ProposedEndEffectorPoseSequence, \ProposedGripperCommandSequence, \text{model\_id}, \text{lease\_id}, \ProposerConfidenceScore, \text{crc} \rangle\] The capture and validity times let the receiver compare the motion with freshness and calibration requirements it establishes independently; for the conveyor pick, \(\ProposalExpiryInstant\) can lie no later than \(\EvidenceCaptureInstant + \EvidenceHorizon\). The payload requests motion. It is neither execution permission nor the handoff evidence record, and a payload that omits its observation time or validity horizon invites the receiver to execute a proposal computed from a scene that no longer exists.

The fields in table 3 illustrate the information needed to interpret a candidate trajectory, rather than prescribing a second wire format. The proposer supplies trajectory and observation information. Validity claims require supporting evidence, while the receiver applies the separately established chunk lease. A lease_id associates the request with that permission process; it does not let the proposer grant itself authority. The header and handoff record specify delivery, admission, and expiration.

↪ Byte layout: The wire format that carries a chunk’s evidence time, expiry, horizon, and joint setpoints, with offsets, types, and checks, is given in Proposal header and chunk payload.

Checkpoint 3.2: Chunk supply and evidence horizons

Before analyzing runtime failure modes, check that you can separate what a chunk supplies from how long the evidence behind it holds.

3.6 Cognitive Vulnerabilities

A chunk proposed by a mobile manipulator’s policy can arrive within its 40 ms execution budget, with every joint setpoint smooth and within kinematic limits, and yet still collide the gripper into the rim of the mug. The trajectory meets its real-time deadline, but its physical content is wrong, and the most consequential failures of learned physical AI systems sit in that gap. Learned foundation models expand task coverage across open-world environments, but no general proof of their integrated, closed-loop behavior across unconstrained state spaces is available. Engineering practice must instead analyze verified components within explicitly bounded operating regions.

Without a verified input domain and sensitivity bound, small sensing changes to a learned proposer can yield unexpectedly large changes in proposed motion, a sensitivity demonstrated early by imperceptible image perturbations that flipped image classification outputs (Szegedy et al. 2013). Benchmark success does not establish closed-loop stability, timing bounds, or physical safety under unseen operating conditions; those guarantees require independent evidence across the model, the software runtime, and the physical plant.9 Even when a valid sensitivity bound exists, it characterizes only the relationship between input perturbations and output deviations over a specified domain. It provides no proof that the nominal proposed action is collision-free or physically admissible, meaning analytical bounds can inform, but never replace, an independent permission decision.

Szegedy, Christian, Wojciech Zaremba, Ilya Sutskever, Joan Bruna, Dumitru Erhan, Ian Goodfellow, and Rob Fergus. 2013. “Intriguing Properties of Neural Networks.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1312.6199.

9 Lipschitz Continuity in Neural Policies: Although neural networks with smooth activations are continuous on compact domains, a sensitivity bound \(\PolicyLipschitzBound = \sup_{\MechanicalPlantState_1 \ne \MechanicalPlantState_2} \frac{\lVert \pi(\MechanicalPlantState_1) - \pi(\MechanicalPlantState_2) \rVert}{\lVert \MechanicalPlantState_1 - \MechanicalPlantState_2 \rVert}\) must be established over a stated operating region. Where that bound holds, sensor error \(\lVert \FiniteChange \MechanicalPlantState \rVert \le \SensorErrorBound\) bounds the action change by \(\lVert \FiniteChange \CognitivePolicyAction \rVert \le \PolicyLipschitzBound\SensorErrorBound\); an unverified \(\PolicyLipschitzBound\) gives no usable action-error guarantee. Full derivations appear in Lipschitz continuity and spectral normalization.

A numerical interface easily traps malformed tensors, NaN values, and out-of-range floating-point setpoints, but it cannot detect a wrong grasp plan. Both classical pipelines and learned models can emit well-formed yet wrong commands; learning, however, adds failures rooted in high-dimensional latent features, out-of-distribution hallucinations, and uncalibrated confidence scores. Independent safety monitors can reject proposals that contradict measured geometry or joint limits, but they cannot infer true task intent. Safe operation in the physical world requires examining each of these failure modes and stating the evidence checks needed beyond packet formatting.

3.6.1 Semantic hallucinations

Consider a mobile manipulator clearing items near a conveyor. A discarded shipping carton carries a high-resolution printed photograph of a ceramic mug. The robot’s vision backbone attends to the visual texture, and the policy proposes an end-effector trajectory that closes the parallel jaws around the printed handle. The trajectory tensor is numerically flawless: smooth, within joint velocity and acceleration bounds, and clear of modeled obstacles. The handle it targets, however, is flat ink on cardboard. In web-scale vision models, attention maps track statistical correlations across 2D pixel arrays rather than measured 3D geometry.

Independent geometric evidence exposes this hallucination directly. A runtime validator compares the proposed grasp pose against calibrated depth returns to check whether physical relief supports the handle. If the depth sensor shows a flat cardboard surface within measurement resolution, the runtime rejects the proposal; if sensor dropouts or occlusion leave the region uncertain, the grasp remains unverified and must not be admitted. In neither case can a 2D attention map stand in for a 3D depth measurement. The proposer must therefore supply both its target coordinate frame and the supporting observation timestamps so that downstream checks can separate a physically grounded motion plan from an unverified visual guess.

↪ How depth error is bounded and qualified before a spatial claim can be trusted is developed in Spatial Error and Clearance Bounds.

3.6.2 The softmax illusion and out-of-distribution overconfidence

A natural temptation is to filter out hallucinations like the cardboard mug, or frames degraded by intense specular glare, dynamic shadows, and water droplets on camera lenses, by rejecting proposals whose reported confidence falls below a threshold. That defense fails when deployment interfaces conflate mathematical normalization with the physical probability of task success. In discrete classification, classification-based tokenization, or categorical action heads, raw output logits are transformed via the softmax operator:10 \[\SoftmaxFunction(\LogitVector)_i = \frac{e^{\OutputLogit_i}}{\sum_{j=1}^\ClassCount e^{\OutputLogit_j}}\] whose outputs sum to one purely by algebraic construction. When an embodied machine encounters those out-of-distribution (OOD) observations or novel visual textures, the network can still emit confidence scores near one for physically disastrous actions. Modern deep networks are often overconfident even on in-distribution inputs (Guo et al. 2017), and networks constructed from rectified linear units provably approach maximum confidence on inputs far from their training distribution (Hein et al. 2019).

10 Softmax (soft arg max): Bridle (1990) named the normalized exponential “softmax” because it is a differentiable version of the winner-take-all operation that picks the largest input. A function built to pick a winner keeps doing so far from its training data, pushing its top output toward one whenever one logit separates from the rest, whether or not the input resembles anything the model has seen.

Bridle, John S. 1990. “Probabilistic Interpretation of Feedforward Classification Network Outputs, with Relationships to Statistical Pattern Recognition.” In Neurocomputing: Algorithms, Architectures and Applications, edited by Françoise Fogelman Soulié and Jeanny Hérault, vol. 68. NATO ASI Series f: Computer and Systems Sciences. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-76153-9_28.
Guo, Chuan, Geoff Pleiss, Yu Sun, and Kilian Q. Weinberger. 2017. “On Calibration of Modern Neural Networks.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 70: 1321–30.
Hein, Matthias, Maksym Andriushchenko, and Julian Bitterwolf. 2019. “Why ReLU Networks Yield High-Confidence Predictions Far Away from the Training Data and How to Mitigate the Problem.” IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR).

Calibration evaluates how closely a model’s predicted confidence aligns with observed empirical success rates under specified conditions. A confidence score is useful for selecting or abstaining from a proposal only to the extent that this relationship holds for the deployed task. Softmax overconfidence and calibration error formalizes binned expected calibration error and post-hoc temperature scaling. From a systems perspective, the execution runtime must bind calibration metadata directly to the model identity (model_id), preventing supervisory layers from mistaking uncalibrated algebraic certainty for physical permission.

In modern physical AI systems, populating the action payload’s confidence field is rarely as straightforward as extracting a softmax probability, because contemporary chunking policies do not operate as discrete classifiers. Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT), for example, decodes each multi-step chunk deterministically at inference time by clamping its latent style variable to the prior mean (Zhao et al. 2023). Similarly, diffusion and flow-matching policies iteratively denoise trajectories from Gaussian noise vectors. Neither architecture natively emits a normalized score. For these continuous decoders, confidence must be derived through an explicit, qualified estimation technique. Three primary architectures are used in practice:

  • Sample dispersion. The system draws multiple trajectory chunks for the identical sensory observation by varying random diffusion noise seeds or sampling latent vectors. Measuring geometric dispersion across these trajectories describes the policy’s sampled output variability. Wide dispersion can reflect several valid motions as well as uncertainty; it does not by itself isolate uncertainty caused by missing knowledge.
  • Ensemble disagreement. Multiple independently trained policy heads evaluate the same sensory input simultaneously. Downstream monitors measure the variance among their trajectory proposals, treating significant disagreement as an indicator of model uncertainty.
  • Conformal thresholds. To convert heuristic dispersion or disagreement metrics into actionable statistical guarantees, the runtime applies inductive conformal prediction. Using a held-out calibration dataset, the system establishes an empirical threshold such that, under the assumption of exchangeability,11 the predicted set covers the calibrated target with a chosen marginal coverage probability. Specifically, each candidate trajectory receives a nonconformity score measuring its residual error or disagreement, and the conformal set collects all candidates whose scores fall within the quantile threshold measured on the calibration trials, so high sensory ambiguity widens the set to include distinct trajectories while pairing the threshold with an admissibility filter can leave no candidate in the set at all. Physical validity still requires independent checks against current geometry and plant limits. If the conformal set is empty or contains mutually conflicting actions, the runtime refuses the candidate or abstains; if active motion is no longer admissible, it requires a physically qualified response or operator intervention (Ren et al. 2023; Angelopoulos and Bates 2023).

11 Exchangeability: The conformal guarantee assumes only that the calibration trials and the new trial are exchangeable, a weaker condition than independent and identically distributed data (Vovk et al. 2005). A deployed robot can break even that weaker assumption because its actions change the scenes it meets next, the endogenous data of the second law (The Four Bedrock Laws). A threshold calibrated under one policy therefore has to be re-established after every policy or deployment change, and its stated coverage lapses in between.

Vovk, Vladimir, Alex Gammerman, and Glenn Shafer. 2005. Algorithmic Learning in a Random World. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/b106715.
Ren, Allen Z., Anushri Dixit, Alexandra Bodrova, Sumeet Singh, Stephen Tu, Noah Brown, Peng Xu, et al. 2023. “Robots That Ask for Help: Uncertainty Alignment for Large Language Model Planners.” Proceedings of the 7th Conference on Robot Learning, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 229: 661–82.
Angelopoulos, Anastasios N., and Stephen Bates. 2023. “Conformal Prediction: A Gentle Introduction.” Foundations and Trends in Machine Learning 16 (4): 494–591. https://doi.org/10.1561/2200000101.

Each uncertainty estimation strategy incurs distinct hardware and memory-bandwidth costs. Generating multiple samples from a single observation can reuse visual backbone features and batch each decoding iteration through the policy head, sharing a weight read across samples. Iterative decoders still revisit their weights across refinement steps, and the balance between arithmetic and memory traffic depends on the batch and kernels. In contrast, an ensemble of disparate models requires reading separate sets of weights across the memory bus for every head, multiplying memory bandwidth traffic by the number of heads. The action payload must therefore treat the confidence scalar \(\ProposerConfidenceScore\) as meaningful only when accompanied by the identifier of the method that produced it and the corresponding calibration record, both resolved through model_id. A model lacking a qualified estimation pipeline must omit the confidence score entirely rather than emit an ungrounded default value.

The stipulated values in table 4 illustrate how environmental and hardware shifts can open a gap between confidence and success; they describe nominal illustrative regimes rather than a single deployed benchmark. The near-OOD row is a covariate shift, a change in the inputs the deployed model meets while the task stays the same. Across all shifted regimes, reported confidence significantly outstrips actual task success. A supervisory filter relying solely on an uncalibrated score threshold would mistakenly admit hazardous trajectories. Choosing an appropriate supervisory response depends on the integrity of the remaining sensor streams and the physical capabilities of the machine; a confidence discrepancy alone cannot dictate whether the robot should slow down, collect additional sensor frames, or execute an emergency stop.

3.6.3 Perceptual chattering and covariate shift

When sensory observations hover near a boundary between competing object classes or grasp modes, small frame-to-frame noise flips the winning label back and forth, a failure known as perceptual chattering. If a motion planner treats each label flip as an abrupt change in the physical world, it discards and resets its trajectory on every frame. In the fatal 2018 Tempe autonomous vehicle collision, the National Transportation Safety Board record documents this failure in the automated driving system: the perception module alternated between classifying a pedestrian pushing a bicycle as a vehicle, a bicycle, and “other.” After each reclassification the path predictor dropped the object’s earlier locations, preventing the motion planner from seeing that the obstacle was crossing the vehicle’s path (National Transportation Safety Board 2019). Covariate shift, the near-OOD regime of table 4 in which the inputs change while the task stays the same, pushes more frames toward those boundaries; Mechanics of Endogenous Covariate Shift develops it.

National Transportation Safety Board. 2019. Collision Between Vehicle Controlled by Developmental Automated Driving System and Pedestrian, Tempe, Arizona, March 18, 2018. HAR-19/03. National Transportation Safety Board.

↩︎ The tracking-history loss under classification changes in the 2018 Tempe collision is established in Tracking history loss under classification churn.

In robotic manipulation, perceptual chattering injects rapid direction reversals into joint trajectory setpoints. Over a discrete control interval \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\), a sharp step in commanded velocity demands an instantaneous acceleration \(\FiniteChange \GeneralizedVelocities / \FiniteChange \TimeInstant\) beyond what the actuators can deliver. PD joint controllers react to the resulting velocity errors by commanding large opposing motor torques, the same derivative-gain torque step that the temporal-ensembling jerk trap of section 3.5.1 priced at a single chunk seam, now repeated at every flip. The resulting current spikes, tracking errors, and heat strain the power stage and gearbox, risking protective drive faults or structural vibration. Physical AI runtimes suppress perceptual chattering with three defenses:

  1. Temporal hysteresis and debouncing. Requiring a newly observed class or spatial mode to persist across \(\DebounceFrameCount\) consecutive perception frames before committing a state change, just like switch debouncing12 in embedded firmware.
  2. Recursive state filtering. Fusing successive observations into a running estimate so that a single noisy frame cannot overwrite an established world state.
  3. Rate and jerk limiting. Enforcing joint acceleration and jerk bounds in the real-time controller so that high-level label flicker cannot reach the motor shafts as torque shocks.

12 Debouncing: The contacts of a mechanical switch bounce for milliseconds when pressed, so embedded firmware accepts a new switch state only after it has persisted for a set interval. The same \(\DebounceFrameCount\)-frame rule applied to class labels suppresses chattering, but it delays every genuine change by \(\DebounceFrameCount\) frames, time charged to the observation-age budget before any proposal is renewed.

↪ Recursive state estimation, which accumulates evidence across frames instead of trusting the latest one, is developed in Estimating State from Dated Measurements.

3.6.4 When intent expires

Even when perception neither hallucinates nor chatters, the physical scene continues to move after a valid goal is issued. Notebook 3.3 already showed a chunk outliving its evidence, and the same failure applies one level up, to intent. Renewing a high-level goal identifier, or retransmitting a previous trajectory, does not refresh the observation that placed the mug. High-capacity intent models, which deliberate over long time horizons at low update rates, must therefore provide goals whose validity can be continuously audited against fresh, real-time telemetry while high-frequency reactive policies stream immediate motion.

The execution receiver must distinguish between rejecting a stale proposal and terminating an active, executing trajectory. Dropping an incoming proposal that failed its arrival deadline or validity check does not automatically invalidate the chunk currently running on the motors. The active chunk continues executing so long as its own chunk lease, the intent lease governing its target, the evidence behind it, its state bounds, and its safety margins all remain valid. If the active chunk lease or the governing intent lease expires, or the evidence behind the active chunk lapses, the supervisory permission path must end nominal tracking and engage the qualified fallback unless a valid successor restores the required authority. Because holding position, decelerating to a controlled stop, and removing torque require distinct physical preconditions, The Fallback Ladder develops the formal fallback ladder across these operational regimes.

Each of the four vulnerabilities ends the same way. Hallucination, overconfidence, chattering, and expiry cannot be reliably ruled out by the proposer’s own output and require checks against independently qualified evidence, which is why the opening comparison of classical and learned methods resolves into a partition rather than a choice. Physical AI architectures assign semantic interpretation to the learned proposer and execution authority to the permission path (table 5). Learned foundation models supply flexible task coverage across unstructured environments, but their lack of closed-loop formal guarantees requires treating their outputs as unprivileged proposals. Conversely, classical analytical checks enforce strict physical invariances and real-time bounds, yet their guarantees remain conditional on accurate kinematic models and fresh sensor data. Safety therefore rests on the interface, which must never let an unverified proposal bypass the checks of the permission path.

Checkpoint 3.3: Proposals that arrive on time and are wrong

A proposal can meet every deadline and still target the wrong thing, so the receiver needs evidence the proposer cannot supply about itself.

3.7 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Learned models expand the behavioral repertoire of robotic systems, but their predictions remain ungrounded hypotheses until verified against physical dynamics and environmental constraints. In deployed systems, critical failures emerge when software architectures treat statistical confidence, numerical formatting, or kinematic smoothness as an unconditional license to command hardware. Avoiding these hazards requires separating candidate generation from deterministic safety enforcement.

Fallacy: An engineering team must choose entirely between formal classical safety proofs and learned empirical capability.

Engineers often frame robot control as a mutual exclusion: either adopt a classical controller with provable stability guarantees, or deploy an expressive neural policy that achieves broad task coverage through empirical learning. Consider an assembly plant routing flexible wiring harnesses. A classical model-based controller enforces rigid kinematic constraints and workspace boundaries, yet it struggles to model the nonlinear dynamics of deformable cables. Conversely, a learned diffusion policy navigates cable deformation successfully, but occasionally proposes motions that exceed physical clearance limits.

The proposal boundary dissolves this dilemma. The diffusion policy proposes, and an independent minimal-intervention filter corrects any command that would cross a clearance limit before it reaches the actuators. The correction preserves the modeled safety constraint without guaranteeing completion of the harness insertion, so systems architects must track task completion, proposer error distribution, and protective intervention frequency as independent performance metrics.

↪ The minimal-intervention filter, which replaces an inadmissible command with the smallest correction that satisfies the physical constraints, is developed in The Minimal Intervention Principle and Optimization Filters.

Pitfall: Allowing a neural model’s high confidence to bypass deterministic geometric boundaries.

A cleanroom manipulator handling reagent vials mistakes specular glare on a transparent glass partition for open workspace and plans a reach directly through the barrier. The resulting action packet satisfies standard transport validation: joint coordinates are finite and in range, the command arrives on schedule, and the neural proposer reports an output confidence of \(\ProposerConfidenceScore = 0.98\). However, a high score indicates only that one output dominated the alternatives. Section 3.6.2 showed that a network can be most confident on inputs far from anything it was trained on. The same specular reflection that deceived the perceptual front-end also corrupts the confidence head, producing an assertive proposal for an impossible motion.

Actuation permission must always derive from deterministic geometric validation rather than statistical self-assessment. Before energizing motor drives, the real-time execution monitor must validate candidate trajectories against qualified local geometric boundaries derived from independent sensors, such as acoustic rangefinders, time-of-flight sensors, or calibrated structural keep-out zones. Because an isolated microcontroller or real-time checker lacks the memory and compute budget to evaluate a full host-resident 3D occupancy grid, the boundary handoff contract must deliver explicit spatial bounds along with verified validity intervals before motors receive current.

Fallacy: Raw softmax probabilities or predicted confidence scores reflect true physical success rates.

An industrial bin-picking policy can assign near-unity scores to grasp candidates that fail immediately under altered lighting or surface conditions. The softmax function merely transforms unbounded real-valued logits into an artificial probability distribution that sums to one across the discrete candidate set. This mathematical normalization quantifies relative preference across the model’s training distribution rather than mechanical stability in physical contact.

Post-hoc calibration methods, such as temperature scaling, align predicted probabilities with empirical accuracy only while operating under conditions identical to the calibration distribution. As the illustrative gaps in table 4 depict, calibration can degrade sharply when lighting, surface friction, or geometry shifts out of distribution. Relying on an abstention threshold applied to raw model scores will silently allow unstable grasps or collisions during operational shifts. Production systems must backstop model proposals with independent physical verification, including tactile slip sensing, force-torque thresholds, and deterministic geometric checks.

Pitfall: Coupling high-frequency semantic classifications directly to motor setpoints without continuous filtering.

A delivery robot navigates past reflective tarps flapping over traffic cones. At every perception update (\(20\,\text{Hz}\)), the vision pipeline alternates its discrete classification between an open corridor, a rigid obstacle, and a compliant drape. If the motion planner maps these discrete classification switches directly into motor setpoints, perceptual noise commands instantaneous velocity reversals. Each reversal is a step in velocity reference, which a PD servo loop turns into the derivative-gain torque step that the temporal ensembling jerk trap computed for a single chunk seam, repeated at every flip. Chattering commands of this kind saturate motor drive currents, cause mechanical backlash wear, and excite dangerous structural resonances throughout the chassis.

Physical architectures must decouple discrete semantic classification from continuous motor control. Engineers introduce temporal debouncing, Bayesian belief filters, or hysteresis thresholds to suppress semantic switching noise. Furthermore, the permission path’s interpolator must hold setpoints within explicit acceleration and jerk limits before dispatching them to the drives. Even when the resulting trajectory appears smooth, the real-time execution engine must verify dynamic feasibility against hardware torque and thermal limits.

Fallacy: A numerically valid and smooth action chunk can safely be executed whenever it arrives.

Modern action-chunking architectures generate sequences of future setpoints to bridge the rate mismatch between slow neural inference on an application processor and fast motor control loops. Consider a mobile manipulator grasping an item from a moving conveyor. A host policy computes a smooth, multi-step arm trajectory toward the target, but operating system scheduling jitter, memory paging, or bus stalls delay packet delivery beyond the sensor evidence horizon. When the action chunk finally reaches the motor controller, its waypoints remain mathematically continuous and within joint kinematic limits. Meanwhile, conveyor motion has shifted the actual object outside the grasp envelope.

Executing an outdated chunk causes the arm to track a phantom path. A motor controller must reject stale packets regardless of their internal smoothness. Action chunks must carry explicit timestamps and temporal validity leases tied to the freshness of their underlying sensor observations. The real-time receiver may execute an active trajectory only while its lease remains valid. If an expected chunk fails to arrive before the lease expires, the controller must execute a qualified deceleration or compliant hold to keep the hardware within safe operational limits. Kinematic smoothness cannot compensate for expired temporal validity.

3.8 Summary

Learned representations give the mobile manipulator what hand-authored geometry could not, the ability to recognize and act on objects no engineer enumerated, and the machine pays for that breadth in memory traffic, arithmetic, and time. A fresh evaluation splits into a compute-bound prefill and a bandwidth-bound decode, so weight-only quantization shortens one term and leaves the other, and a large intent model on an embedded processor renews only a few times per second. Supply and validity are therefore separate budgets. A chunk keeps the arm supplied between renewals, but every setpoint in it descends from one observation, so the time the target stays within tolerance, not the length of the chunk, bounds how much of it may run. What the slow proposer hands the fast one decides what the permission path can check, and because a proposal can arrive on time and still be wrong, every proposal must carry its capture time, validity limit, and provenance so that it remains refusable. Six core quantitative relations govern these trade-offs across the chapter, each bounding a distinct stage of proposal generation and expiration (table 6).

Key Takeaways: Learned proposers and deliberation limits
  • Memory traffic and arithmetic both bound fresh inference: When an evaluation cycle streams nonresident weights from memory, the total weight footprint and sustained memory bandwidth establish a hard latency floor. Fetching the intent model’s 14 GB of FP16 weights requires 98.0 ms. Input token prefill operates in the compute-bound regime, whereas per-token autoregressive decoding is bandwidth-bound. Consequently, even with INT4 weights, a single evaluation consumes approximately 336 ms. This supports the selected intent renewal rate of 2 Hz, which is far too slow to drive real-time actuator chunk renewal directly.
  • Chunking supplies motion without refreshing evidence: Generating a chunk of 16 setpoints amortizes a single backbone evaluation across 400 ms of candidate motion. Although this amortizes memory bus traffic across waypoints, the robot executes that motion without incorporating new visual evidence until the subsequent policy evaluation completes.
  • Supply and validity bound different horizons: In the conveyor pick task, actuator continuity requires a minimum supply horizon of 4 setpoints to bridge neural renewal intervals, whereas target drift constrains the sensory evidence horizon to approximately 219 ms. A deeper queue of setpoints cannot extend that validity. A setpoint stays eligible only while the evidence behind it holds and the active chunk lease runs, and when renewal stops, the chunk lease bounds how far the arm continues.
  • Retained context competes with current sensing: Retaining the hypothetical five-second history at the stated sampling rate occupies 21.0 GB of key-value cache memory. Because storing and rereading past attention state directly contends for DRAM bandwidth with incoming sensory observations and proposal generation, temporal context retention must be explicitly managed within the system timing and memory budget.
  • Proposer interfaces dictate runtime verifiability: An opaque latent embedding passed from a high-level intent model to a low-level policy cannot be cross-referenced with measured workspace geometry or aged against target drift. Conversely, an explicit Cartesian target pose accompanied by an observation timestamp provides a checkable contract. This auditable geometric interface allows the machine’s permission path to enforce its intent lease alongside the high-rate chunk lease.
  • Reported confidence requires independent physical validation: A softmax probability or normalized confidence score emitted by a learned model cannot guarantee physical execution success under real-world distribution shift. While calibration validates score reliability across known operating regimes, runtime safety demands independent geometric constraints, kinematic limits, and physical contact monitoring.
  • Proposals must remain rejectable: Trajectory proposals must bundle explicit observation timestamps, kinematic assumptions, and validity envelopes alongside their raw setpoints. The Nervous System combines these proposer constraints with real-time body state measurements to validate trajectory headers, manage handoffs between successive chunks, and enforce fail-safe execution windows.

The conveyor pick separates two kinds of inadequacy that call for different fixes. A proposer that cannot renew before its evidence expires has design remedies: fewer input tokens, a smaller or differently quantized model, more bandwidth, or a slower target. A proposer that renews in time and still proposes a wrong grasp is not repaired by any of them, which is why its output stays a proposal however fast it arrives. Both cases end at the first law (principle 1). Motion executed on evidence that has gone stale, or toward an affordance that does not exist, delivers energy that cannot be recalled. The time a proposal may keep driving the machine therefore has to be set below the proposer, from the body’s stopping budget, rather than by the proposer’s own schedule.

3.9 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The two-speed architecture developed throughout this chapter, which couples a slow deliberative proposer with a high-frequency execution loop that continuously services the actuators, is robotics’ recurring answer to a timing constraint. Physical machines have wrestled with this division since Shakey’s deliberative planning (Nilsson 1984), Brooks’ reactive subsumption layers (Brooks 1986), and ALVINN’s direct learned steering (Pomerleau 1989). The classical industrial pipelines examined in section 3.2 retained explicit geometric representations by restricting robot operation to structured, calibrated workcells where rigid kinematic models hold, while end-to-end learning gained broad behavioral coverage from data at the cost of formal analytical guarantees.

Nilsson, Nils J. 1984. Shakey the Robot. Technical Note No. 323. Artificial Intelligence Center, SRI International.
Pomerleau, Dean A. 1989. “ALVINN: An Autonomous Land Vehicle in a Neural Network.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 1: 305–13.

↩︎ The lineage from Shakey’s deliberative planning through Brooks’ subsumption layers to ALVINN’s learned steering is established in From Prediction to Action.

Vaswani, Ashish, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez, Łukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. 2017. “Attention Is All You Need.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30.
Jacob, Benoit, Skirmantas Kligys, Bo Chen, Menglong Zhu, Matthew Tang, Andrew Howard, Hartwig Adam, and Dmitry Kalenichenko. 2018. “Quantization and Training of Neural Networks for Efficient Integer-Arithmetic-Only Inference.” 2018 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition, 2704–13. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2018.00286.
Lin, Ji, Jiaming Tang, Haotian Tang, Shang Yang, Wei-Ming Chen, Wei-Chen Wang, Guangxuan Xiao, Xingyu Dang, Chuang Gan, and Song Han. 2024. “AWQ: Activation-Aware Weight Quantization for on-Device LLM Compression and Acceleration.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 6: 87–100.

The hardware bottlenecks that govern modern policy inference stem from a parallel evolution in computer architecture. Wulf and McKee (1995) identified the widening memory wall, observing that processor speed was improving far faster than DRAM access time. Williams et al. (2009) formalized this relationship into the roofline model, establishing operational arithmetic intensity, the ratio of floating-point operations performed to memory bytes transferred, as the metric that reveals whether an execution phase is memory-bandwidth-bound or compute-bound. Modern autoregressive models collide directly with these bandwidth ceilings. The Transformer architecture requires every newly generated token to attend over cached keys and values from all preceding context tokens (Vaswani et al. 2017). As Pope et al. (2023) demonstrated through analytical modeling of distributed Transformer inference, generating one token at a time splits execution into a compute-bound prompt prefill phase, which processes all input tokens together, and a memory-bandwidth-bound autoregressive decoding phase. In embodied setups, where real-time interactive control requires generating action tokens with a batch size of 1, the memory wall dominates the decode, as notebook 3.1 prices for the machine and checks against a published measurement on its processor. To alleviate this memory traffic, integer-only quantization demonstrated that low-precision arithmetic could run efficiently on mobile processors with small accuracy loss on the vision networks it evaluated (Jacob et al. 2018). As the intent-model measurements of table 1 show, however, the cost of a given bit width depends on the method, the kernels, and the task. Modern weight-only formats such as AWQ protect salient weight channels while cutting the bytes transferred across the memory bus on each decoding pass (Lin et al. 2024).

Learned proposers emerged when these sequence architectures were adapted to robot sensorimotor trajectories. The Vision Transformer demonstrated that self-attention could replace convolutional feature extractors on raw image patches without losing spatial coherence (Dosovitskiy et al. 2021). RT-1 and RT-2 extended this foundation to robotics by tokenizing continuous physical actions directly into the vocabulary of large vision-language models (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023; Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Chen, et al. 2023), while OpenVLA established an open, reproducible seven-billion-parameter architecture that enabled systematic hardware profiling and fine-tuning (Kim et al. 2024). However, evaluating large transformer backbones autoregressively at every single control step proved computationally impractical for low-latency physical loops. Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) and Diffusion Policy resolved this bottleneck by shifting the unit of prediction from isolated steps to short trajectories (Zhao et al. 2023; Chi et al. 2025), amortizing inference latency over multiple timesteps and reducing the compounding of per-step errors. Advancing this approach, \(\pi_0\) coupled a high-capacity vision-language backbone with a flow-matching action expert capable of generating high-frequency motor commands (Black et al. 2024). Real-time action chunking hid the inference delay behind execution by freezing the setpoints that run during inference and generating the rest of the next chunk to agree with them (Black, Galliker, et al. 2025), a formulation that recasts classical receding-horizon model predictive control through the lens of deep learning (Mayne et al. 2000). Modern embodied platforms, including Helix (Figure AI 2025) and GR00T N1 (Bjorck et al. 2025), adopt the same two-speed separation. The contract between the two tiers remains an open design question. Deployed systems pass latent vectors, language, or geometry from the slow model to the fast one (table 2). This chapter’s case for a checkable geometric target, together with the invariants a real-time monitor must enforce on it, is one position in that question rather than a settled standard.

Dosovitskiy, Alexey, Lucas Beyer, Alexander Kolesnikov, Dirk Weissenborn, Xiaohua Zhai, Thomas Unterthiner, Mostafa Dehghani, et al. 2021. “An Image Is Worth 16x16 Words: Transformers for Image Recognition at Scale.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Joseph Dabis, Chelsea Finn, Keerthana Gopalakrishnan, et al. 2023. “RT-1: Robotics Transformer for Real-World Control at Scale.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.025.
Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Xi Chen, Krzysztof Choromanski, Tianli Ding, et al. 2023. “RT-2: Vision-Language-Action Models Transfer Web Knowledge to Robotic Control.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.15818.
Black, Kevin, Noah Brown, Danny Driess, Adnan Esmail, Michael Equi, Chelsea Finn, Niccolo Fusai, et al. 2024. “\(\pi_0\): A Vision-Language-Action Flow Model for General Robot Control.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2410.24164.
Black, Kevin, Manuel Y. Galliker, and Sergey Levine. 2025. “Real-Time Execution of Action Chunking Flow Policies.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS).
Figure AI. 2025. Helix: A Vision-Language-Action Model for Generalist Humanoid Control. Figure AI technical report (web).
Bjorck, Johan, Fernando Castañeda, Nikita Cherniadev, Xingye Da, Runyu Ding, Linxi Fan, Yu Fang, et al. 2025. “GR00T N1: An Open Foundation Model for Generalist Humanoid Robots.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2503.14734.

Six works trace this lineage from reactive robotics to modern vision-language-action architectures:

  • Brooks (1986) introduces the subsumption architecture, organizing autonomous robots into layered, asynchronous behaviors that each close their own sensorimotor loop. The paper presents the foundational systems argument that fast reactive loops must never block on deliberative planning, establishing the conceptual basis for modern two-speed execution.
  • Williams et al. (2009) introduces the roofline model, a visual bound relating arithmetic intensity to processor compute and memory bandwidth ceilings. The paper provides the standard methodology for diagnosing whether an inference kernel is memory-bound or compute-bound before undertaking hardware optimization.
  • Pope et al. (2023) models Transformer inference from first principles, demonstrating how batch size, sequence length, and tensor partitioning dictate whether execution binds on memory bandwidth or arithmetic execution units. This analysis underpins the performance characterization of batch-size-1 policy inference explored throughout this chapter.
  • Zhao et al. (2023) introduces Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) and temporal ensembling for precise bimanual manipulation. The authors demonstrate that predicting action sequences rather than individual steps dampens compounding tracking errors and stabilizes learned policies, establishing the trajectory chunking approach that real-time learned policies now rely on.
  • Chi et al. (2025) develops Diffusion Policy, demonstrating why multimodal human demonstrations with multiple valid solutions require expressive generative decoders rather than unimodal regression. The paper explicitly characterizes the systems cost of iterative refinement, quantifying the computational latency required to evaluate multiple diffusion steps per action chunk.
  • Kim et al. (2024) provides a complete, open-source seven-billion-parameter vision-language-action model, detailing its training pipeline, data mixture, and deployment trade-offs. The paper includes 4-bit and 8-bit quantization benchmarks, which make it a common reference point for profiling learned embodied policies on edge hardware.
Brooks, Rodney. 1986. “A Robust Layered Control System for a Mobile Robot.” IEEE Journal on Robotics and Automation 2 (1): 14–23.
Williams, Samuel, Andrew Waterman, and David Patterson. 2009. “Roofline: An Insightful Visual Performance Model for Multicore Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 52 (4): 65–76.
Pope, Reiner, Sholto Douglas, Aakanksha Chowdhery, Jacob Devlin, James Bradbury, Jonathan Heek, Kefan Xiao, Shivani Agrawal, and Jeff Dean. 2023. “Efficiently Scaling Transformer Inference.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5: 606–24.
Zhao, Tony Z., Vikash Kumar, Sergey Levine, and Chelsea Finn. 2023. “Learning Fine-Grained Bimanual Manipulation with Low-Cost Hardware.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.016.
Chi, Cheng, Zhenjia Xu, Siyuan Feng, Eric Cousineau, Yilun Du, Benjamin Burchfiel, Russ Tedrake, and Shuran Song. 2025. “Diffusion Policy: Visuomotor Policy Learning via Action Diffusion.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 44 (10-11): 1684–704. https://doi.org/10.1177/02783649241273668.
Kim, Moo Jin, Karl Pertsch, Siddharth Karamcheti, Ted Xiao, Ashwin Balakrishna, Suraj Nair, Rafael Rafailov, et al. 2024. “OpenVLA: An Open-Source Vision-Language-Action Model.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2406.09246.

What’s Next: From learned proposals to the permission path
A physical machine cannot allow a candidate proposal to continue driving hardware indefinitely when its successor fails to arrive. The mobile manipulator’s chunk policy targets a proposal period of 50 ms, whereas the intent model updates at a slower cadence, and either level can stall, miss its deadline, or misjudge the target object. A check that waits on the proposer it checks stalls along with it. The Nervous System therefore begins with where the permission path must run to stay independent of the learned models. It next derives each tier’s rate from a physical time constant and passes proposals between tiers without letting a slow writer block a fast reader. Only then does it price the chunk lease from the body’s stopping budget and the proposer’s renewal floor, ensuring that an admitted chunk commands the joints for a bounded interval before a deterministic fail-safe response begins.
